# Compiler-Readiness Plan — Compiler-Only Scope (revised 2026-06-16)

**status:** Draft plan — 2026-06-16 (supersedes `compiler-readiness-plan-2026-06-11.md`, which is retained for diffing; promote to `Locked` only once the Phase-0 design gates clear)
**scope boundary:** COMPILER code + ALL CANONICAL DESIGN (compiler docs `docs/compiler/*` / `docs/language/*` AND runtime canon `docs/runtime/*.md`). Runtime CODING is OUT — deferred to a separate runtime initiative, its design captured here.
**grounding research:** `research/architecture/compiler/fault-floor-definition-2026-06-11.md` — materially revised by the owner decisions D1–D3 (defined below in the terms section and stated in full in § 2); **must be reconciled per the companion `compiler-readiness-plan-2026-06-16-design-revision-notes.md` before it grounds any slice** — the research doc predates decisions D1–D3, and a grounding document that predates a later decision carries stale framing, so it must be reconciled first rather than handed to any slice as-is.
**design-revision companion:** `compiler-readiness-plan-2026-06-16-design-revision-notes.md` — the concrete fault-floor research-doc edits + the consolidated runtime-canon capture-obligation list.

## Terms used in this plan

One sentence per term (occasionally two where a name needs disambiguating), ordered so a cold reader top-to-bottom always has the vocabulary before it is used; the pointer after each shows where the term was decided or is canonically defined. Pointers beginning "Plan §" refer to sections of this plan; pointers naming a file refer to that document. General language/pipeline terms (rule, ensure, catalog, obligation, SemanticIndex, …) are defined in `docs/glossary.md` and are not repeated here except where this plan gives them a load-bearing role.

### Plan sections cited by shorthand

Four sections of this plan are cited by shorthand throughout; resolve them here first.

- **§11 (Runtime-Coding Handoff)** — the plan section that enumerates every piece of deferred runtime coding, one row per item, each with a capture pointer; "written into §11" means added to that enumeration, not built. *(Plan §11.)*
- **Coverage Ledger (§A–§H)** — this plan's own accounting section (immediately after §11) that reconciles every input finding list against the plan; its eight subsections §A–§H are the eight input registers — floor gaps (§A), scale-backs (§B), registry corrections (§C), spec-coverage gaps (§D), recovered obligations (§E), open questions dropped or half-carried by earlier plans and now explicitly re-placed by this one (§F), parked items (§G), and the runtime handoff cross-reference (§H, which mirrors plan §11). *(Plan, § "Coverage ledger".)*
- **DoD / DoD-1 … DoD-10** — the ten numbered Definition-of-Done criteria in plan §12; "DoD-4" is the fourth (overflow disclosed as a known limitation). *(Plan §12.)*
- **Sequencing red-team / Stress 1** — the plan section that adversarially stress-tests the plan's ordering claims; Stress 1 is its first test, which verifies that the single hard ordering (see "hard ordering") holds and is the only one. *(Plan, § "Sequencing red-team".)*

### The boundary (§0)

- **Compiler/runtime boundary** — this plan builds and verifies only the compiler; the runtime stays an unimplemented stub, but every runtime design decision the compiler forces is written into `docs/runtime/*.md` so nothing is lost. *(Plan §0.)*
- **Compiler-pure (synonym: compiler-completable)** — two names for the same property: a change or exit criterion is fully verifiable with the runtime still stubbed, via `precept_compile`, `dotnet build`, or `dotnet test`. *(Plan §0, discipline 1.)*
- **Prove-or-reject** — the compiler's permanent behavior for genuine safety questions: if it cannot prove an operation can never fault, it rejects the definition, and a future runtime can never change that verdict (the runtime being absent is never itself a reason to reject). *(Plan §0, discipline 3; grounded in `docs/compiler/proof-engine.md` and `docs/philosophy.md`.)*
- **Admit-now / defer-enforcement** — the treatment for operations that are safe *provided the runtime enforces something*: the compiler accepts them today and the runtime's enforcement job is written into §11 (defined above) rather than built; the compiler never keeps rejecting merely because the runtime is unbuilt. *(Plan §0, discipline 3.)*
- **Capture pointer** — the named `docs/runtime/*.md` section that must hold the design for each piece of deferred runtime work; deferring without one is forbidden. *(Plan §0 discipline 2; §11 discipline 1.)*
- **Silent loss** — a deferred item with no capture pointer, i.e. runtime work that would be forgotten instead of handed off; disallowed. *(Plan §0, discipline 2.)*
- **COMPILER_CODE / CANON_DESIGN / RUNTIME_CODE_DEFER / PARKED** — the four classification labels, exactly one per plan item: compiler implementation / canonical doc-and-design work / runtime coding deferred with a capture pointer / deliberately not done. *(Plan §1.)*
- **Parked** — deliberately not built or not decided, recorded with a reason as a documented known limitation — never silently dropped, never claimed done or prevented. *(Plan §1; D1.)*

### Fault floor & bands (the vocabulary the owner decisions are stated in — read before §2's D1/D2)

- **Fault floor** — the set of runtime failure families (division by zero, null access, empty-collection access, …) the compiler must prove absent or reject the definition; after owner decision D1 (next section) it holds nine families, the two overflow families being parked. *(`research/architecture/compiler/fault-floor-definition-2026-06-11.md`, as reconciled by Slice 0.4.)*
- **Band** — a declared min/max, length, or count bound on a field (the PRE0078/PRE0135/PRE0136 checks); owner decision D2 (next section) reclassifies band violations from faults to warnings on fields whose bounds feed no proof. *(Plan §2, D2.)*
- **Carries-proof** — describes a band whose declared bound actually feeds a compile-time safety proof (e.g. a mincount that discharges collection-access safety); these stay in the fault floor and keep prove-or-reject after D2. *(Plan §2, D2.)*
- **Non-proof-carrying** — the opposite: a band nothing in the proof engine depends on; its violations become compile-time warnings now and runtime governance's job later. *(Plan §2, D2.)*
- **Proven-violation warning** — the warning emitted only when the compiler can actually prove a band is violated; merely-unprovable cases compile clean instead of being rejected. *(Plan §2, D2; Phase 1 Slice 1.3.)*
- **Fault-code registry** — the source tables (`FaultCode.cs` + `StaticallyPreventableMap.cs`) pairing runtime fault codes with the compile-time diagnostics that prevent them; "the registry" always means this. *(Source `FaultCode.cs`, `StaticallyPreventableMap.cs`; `docs/compiler/diagnostic-system.md`.)*
- **Honest registry** — the fault-code registry after Phase 1 Slice 1.4 fixes three dishonesties: codes that describe rule violations rather than runtime faults are no longer listed as statically-prevented faults; every fault family routes to its own diagnostic instead of unmatched cases falling back to the division-by-zero diagnostic; and the code-to-diagnostic map matches the source. *(Plan §2 D3; Phase 1 Slice 1.4.)*
- **Bijective core** — the frozen set pairing each statically-prevented fault code one-to-one with the compile-time diagnostic that prevents it. *(Source `StaticallyPreventableMap.cs:28–37`; `docs/compiler/diagnostic-system.md`.)*
- **[StaticallyPreventable]** — the attribute on a fault code naming the diagnostic that prevents it at compile time; a partner diagnostic that never fires means the guarantee is claimed but unbacked. *(Source `FaultCode.cs`; `docs/compiler/diagnostic-system.md`.)*
- **Defense-in-depth trap** — a runtime fault check kept as a backstop even where a compile-time guarantee stands ("trap" in the runtime sense — a check that halts with a fault — not a pitfall to avoid); for NumericOverflow the trap is deliberately kept with *no* live compile-time guarantee behind it, which is D1's disclosed gap. *(Plan §2, D1 consequence.)*
- **Three-category taxonomy** — every diagnostic belongs to exactly one of: the fault floor (prove-or-reject), definition incoherence, or the flag layer. *(GATE-H — a Phase-0 owner-decision gate, defined in the "Gates, decisions, and registers" section below; Slice 0.2.)*
- **Definition incoherence** — a definition that is wrong regardless of any data (e.g. every possible Create fails); always a compile error, in neither the fault nor the warning category. *(GATE-H, Slice 0.2.)*
- **Flag layer** — the post-runtime layer of warning-severity diagnostics, each of which can individually earn promotion to error once real runtime evidence exists; out of this plan's scope. *(GATE-H; plan §1 PARKED list.)*

### The four owner decisions (§2) — applied, never re-litigated

- **D1 (overflow parked)** — decimal and temporal representability overflow (a computed value exceeding what the number/date types can represent) is an accepted, documented, temporary known fault: no prevention machinery is built for it and no doc may claim it prevented. *(Plan §2, D1.)*
- **D2 (band split)** — band checks are downgraded compiler-side from rejection to the proven-violation warning on non-proof-carrying fields, while runtime enforcement of those bands is deferred to §11; carries-proof bands keep rejecting. (All four terms defined in the previous section.) *(Plan §2, D2.)*
- **D3 (atomicity relaxed)** — because this is pre-release with a stubbed runtime, changes land incrementally with no coordinated all-at-once merges; the single hard ordering kept is that the fault-code registry must be honest (Phase 1 Slice 1.4) before the creation-exhaustiveness checker (Phase 2 Slice 2.3 — "the Phase-2 checker") first reads it. *(Plan §2, D3.)*
- **D4 (review fixes folded)** — the accepted findings of the adversarial review of the prior plan are applied throughout. *(Plan §2, D4.)*
- **Frank / A-numbers / B-numbers** — "Frank" is the adversarial plan review whose findings D4 folds in; A-numbered findings are framing/correctness fixes (e.g. A6), B-numbered ones are factual-accuracy fixes (e.g. B2 wording). *(Plan §2, D4.)*
- **Relocated, not weakened (Frank A6)** — the band change moves prevention from compile-time rejection to future runtime governance for non-proof-carrying bands; prevention is not removed — kept distinct from D1's overflow, which is genuinely relaxed. *(Plan §2, D2 tradeoff; D4 A6.)*
- **Honesty carve-out / known limitation** — D1's posture: the parked overflow families are disclosed as not prevented, so no doc, analyzer string, or guarantee statement may claim otherwise. *(Plan §2, D1; plan §12 DoD-4.)*
- **Hard ordering** — a sequencing constraint whose violation would produce wrong results; the plan has exactly one — honest registry before the creation-exhaustiveness checker — and every other ordering is preference. *(Plan §2, D3; verified in the Sequencing red-team, Stress 1.)*

### Gates, decisions, and registers (plan structure)

- **GATE-A … GATE-O** — the lettered Phase-0 owner-decision gates, each one open design or canon question the owner must rule on; the roster lives in Slices 0.2/0.3, GATE-A is already resolved as "park" (D1), and GATE-M alone resolves later, at Phase-5 start. *(Plan §3, Slices 0.2/0.3.)*
- **D6, D8, D9, D13a, D15–D18, Decision 4/7/17** — numbered decisions already locked in the canonical docs (mostly `docs/language/business-domain-types.md` and `docs/runtime/evaluator.md`); distinct from this plan's D1–D4. *(The owning canonical doc, named at each use.)*
- **Point A / Point B / Point C, Q4a / Q4b, Q5, OD-1** — open questions dropped or half-carried by earlier plans, now each explicitly re-placed: Point A = whether statically-decidable-but-unproven rules are rejected at compile time or left to runtime governance; Point B = whether certain/possible/impossible becomes the guarantee vocabulary; Point C = which outcome type owns each external-input value violation (invalid-argument vs constraint-failure vs fault); Q4a = the warning-severity policy for unsatisfiable/mutual-bound rules; Q4b = whether "runtime checks are only defensive redundancy" is scoped to definition-internal values or also covers externally-sourced inputs; Q5 = giving the compile/runtime division of labor one canonical home; OD-1 = the constraint that the runtime evaluator and compile-time constant folding must share one expression-evaluation core. *(Coverage Ledger §F; Slices 0.2/0.3.)*
- **Register (input register, §A–§H)** — an enumerated input list of findings or obligations this plan must account for; the eight input registers are the Coverage Ledger's subsections §A–§H (defined at top), and every row of every register must land in exactly one disposition. Distinct from the Phase-6 *merged diagnostic register* (Process section below), which reuses the word "register" for an output tracker. *(Coverage Ledger.)*
- **Disposition** — the explicit recorded outcome for one register row (built in phase N / deferred with a capture pointer / parked with reason); "nothing dropped silently" means every row has one. *(Coverage Ledger.)*
- **2c-i / 2c-ii** — the two halves of the locked relational-rules-and-bounds design: 2c-i (already shipped) lets declared rules narrow each other during proof; 2c-ii (Phase 5 Slice 5.1, BUG-020) makes field-referencing bound modifiers participate in proof the same way. *(The locked relational-rules-and-bounds design; `docs/compiler/proof-engine.md`.)*

### Proof engine

- **Obligation / discharge** — an obligation is a safety fact the proof engine must establish (e.g. "this divisor is never zero"); discharging it means proving it from an available fact (a guard, a declared rule, a modifier), and an undischarged obligation rejects the definition. *(`docs/compiler/proof-engine.md`.)*
- **Tier-2 discharge (proof-engine sense)** — discharging an obligation from a standing declared fact (a rule or modifier) rather than from a guard next to the expression. Unrelated to the owner-consultation Tier-1/2/3 (Process section): near proofs, "tier" means discharge source; near owner process, it means consultation depth. *(GATE-B; Phase 3 Slice 3.1; `docs/compiler/proof-engine.md`.)*
- **False-Proved** — a proof-ledger entry recording an obligation as discharged when the fault is not actually ruled out; Phase 3 closes every known one. *(Plan Phase 3.)*
- **Fail-open position** — a child position inside an expression tree (a guard, a `because`-message hole, a member-call argument) that the obligation-creation walk never visits, so a fault there silently collects no obligation. *(Plan Phase 2; BUG-031/032.)*
- **Position-totality** — the Phase-2 property that the obligation-creation walk visits every catalogued child-expression position. *(Plan Phase 2.)*
- **Creation-exhaustiveness checker ("the Phase-2 checker")** — the build-time check that fails the compiler's own build if any obligation family stops being created at any catalogued position, making fail-open positions structurally impossible; it permanently encodes whichever fault-code routing the registry holds the first time it runs, which is why the honest registry must land first (the plan's one hard ordering). *(Phase 2, Slice 2.3; ordering verified in Sequencing red-team, Stress 1.)*
- **Intra-chain (same-transaction)** — writes the compiler can see within a single event's action chain; a standing fact invalidated by such a write must not discharge, and the compiler rejects the case — Slice 3.1's genuine compiler work. *(Slice 3.1 scope boundary.)*
- **Committed-state** — values persisted by earlier, separate operations; discharging proofs against them is admitted now, made sound later by the runtime enforcing each constraint at every prior commit (deferred, §11). *(Slice 3.1 scope boundary.)*
- **Staleness / staleness-invalidation** — the existing discipline that a fact about a field reassigned earlier in the chain is stale and cannot discharge an obligation. *(`docs/compiler/proof-engine.md` sequential proof flow; `ProofLedger.cs`.)*
- **Strategy 2 / Strategy 4** — numbered discharge strategies in the proof engine (Strategy 2 = collection-growth reasoning; Strategy 4 = relations sourced from declared rules). *(`docs/compiler/proof-engine.md`.)*
- **DesugarsToRule** — catalog metadata declaring the rule a modifier is equivalent to; Phase 1 Slice 1.2 turns it into synthesized typed rules in the compiled model. *(Source `Modifiers.cs`; `docs/language/catalog-system.md`.)*
- **Unconsumed-artifact guard** — the rule that those synthesized rules may land only if a real compile-time consumer reads them; otherwise the synthesis is deferred together ("co-deferred") with the runtime sweep. *(Phase 1, Slice 1.2.)*

### Deferred runtime vocabulary

- **Governance sweep (synonyms: constraint sweep, ingress sweep, governance lane)** — the future runtime mechanism that evaluates every declared constraint at each commit and each point where external values enter; entirely deferred, designed as one coherent section in `evaluator.md §7.6`. *(`docs/language/precept-language-spec.md` §0.7; plan §11.A.)*
- **Ingress** — any boundary where externally-supplied values enter a governed entity (event arguments, collection-element writes, restore). *(`docs/runtime/evaluator.md`.)*
- **Recoverable governance refusal** — the runtime outcome for a reclassified band violation: the write is refused and the in-flight copy discarded, as a normal structured outcome rather than a fault. *(Plan §11.A; `docs/runtime/result-types.md` capture.)*
- **Working copy** — the runtime's in-flight copy of an entity's data during an operation, discarded whole on refusal. *(`docs/runtime/evaluator.md`.)*
- **Prospect** — the runtime inspection verdict type (Certain / Possible / Impossible) that deliberately has no fault variant, which is why the compiler must have already closed every guard-position fault. *(Source `Inspection.cs`; `docs/runtime/result-types.md`; Phase 2 Slice 2.5.)*
- **Skip'd runtime facet** — the runtime-behavior portion ("facet") of the conformance test suite, written now as skipped test cases; the cases themselves are the acceptance spec the future runtime build must turn green. *(Phase 7, Slice 7.2.)*
- **Own-lane, allowed-red** — the conformance test project is excluded from the default test green bar so its deliberately failing or skipped cases never block the build. *(Phase 7, Slice 7.1.)*

### Process & verification

- **Compat wave** — the batch of existing sample and fixture files that start correctly failing after a soundness fix lands; inventoried and repaired in Phase 2 Slice 2.4. *(Plan Phase 2.)*
- **Emit-or-retire** — the Phase-6 discipline that every declared diagnostic code either demonstrably fires or is explicitly retired with its enum member removed. *(Phase 6, Slice 6.1.)*
- **Merged diagnostic register ("ONE register")** — the Phase-6 output tracker that collapses the three separate diagnostic-completeness trackers — the emit-or-retire dispositions, the conformance suite's coverage tags, and the emission-ownership audit — into a single source of truth; reuses the word "register" for an output tracker, distinct from the input registers §A–§H. *(Phase 6, Slice 6.7.)*
- **Witness richness** — enriching the explanation attached to a rejected proof (what failed, against which requirement, which strategy was tried); message quality only, never a re-verification pass. *(Phase 6, Slice 6.6.)*
- **Four-leg rationale** — every locked decision records rationale, alternatives rejected, precedent, and the tradeoff accepted. *(CLAUDE.md, Per-Decision Rationale.)*
- **Tier-1 / Tier-2 / Tier-3 (owner-consultation tiers)** — the consultation depths: mechanical work needs no gate; new language surface needs a conversation before design; a conflict with a locked spec decision needs a heavier conversation quoting the prior decision verbatim. Unrelated to "tier-2 discharge" in the proof engine (see that entry). *(CLAUDE.md, Pre-Design Owner Consultation.)*
- **Doc-touch** — the per-slice list of documentation that must be updated in the same pass as the change. *(CLAUDE.md, Documentation Sync.)*

---

## 0. The boundary (read first)

This plan finishes **the compiler**. It deliberately stops at a hard line, and every item in the plan is placed on one side of that line or the other.

- **IN SCOPE — (a) compiler code.** Every change to the lexer, the parser, the type checker, the binder, the graph analyzer, the proof engine, diagnostic emission, the fault-code registry, and the Roslyn analyzer. Each of these changes is verifiable at compile time via `precept_compile` / `dotnet build`. The runtime being a stub never blocks a compiler exit criterion.
- **IN SCOPE — (b) all canonical design/doc work.** This covers both the compiler canon (`docs/compiler/*`, `docs/language/*`) **and** the runtime canon (`docs/runtime/*.md`). Wherever a compiler decision forces a runtime contract — fault delivery, the governance sweep, conversion semantics — that contract's **design is captured here**, so the eventual runtime build inherits a complete spec. Nothing is lost.
- **OUT OF SCOPE — runtime coding.** This means the executable model, the governance/constraint sweep, the five runtime operations (Fire / Update / InspectFire / InspectUpdate / Restore), ingress enforcement, and value-application — the runtime step that writes computed values into the entity's fields. The stub status is verified in source, not assumed. **Verified stub:** `src/Precept/Runtime/Evaluator.cs` — Fire (`:80`), Update (`:99`), InspectFire (`:121`), InspectUpdate (`:132`), Restore (`:155`) all `throw new NotImplementedException()`, each tagged "once the executable model is designed." There is no constraint sweep. There is no governance lane. The executable model is not even designed. Every item whose **correctness or exit criterion requires the runtime to execute** is deferred rather than built — the full enumeration of those deferrals is § 11, the Runtime-Coding Handoff.

### Three boundary disciplines (apply to every item)

1. **Compiler-completable exit criteria.** No exit criterion in this plan asserts a runtime-execution outcome; every one is checkable with the runtime still stubbed. As a concrete example: a band slice's exit criterion is "compiles clean / emits the proven-violation warning" (the warning fired only when the compiler can actually prove the declared bound is violated) — never "governance refuses the write," which only a running runtime could demonstrate.
2. **Mandatory capture obligation per defer.** Every item classified `RUNTIME_CODE_DEFER` — runtime coding deferred rather than built — names the specific `docs/runtime/*.md` document it must revise, so the deferred coding has a spec waiting for it. That named document section is the item's capture pointer. A defer with no capture pointer is a silent loss — runtime work that would be forgotten instead of handed off — and is disallowed.
3. **Prove-or-reject (permanent) and admit-now / defer-enforcement (transient).** These are two distinct policies, and keeping them distinct is the load-bearing point of this discipline. The first is permanent: the compiler rejects any definition it cannot prove **fault-safe** — period. That is prevention-engine behavior and has nothing to do with whether the runtime exists. Runtime-absence is **not** fault-unprovability, so a not-yet-built runtime never converts a fault-safe operation into a ground for rejection. The second is transient and applies to a different class of operation: where an operation **is** fault-safe once the runtime performs its part, the compiler **admits** the operation now and the runtime **enforcement** is deferred and captured in § 11. Three examples of that class: reclassified-band governance, which is the canonical model for this treatment; auto-conversion of a commensurable same-dimension quantity to the target unit, per decision D8 ("Commensurable arithmetic with deterministic unit resolution") in `docs/language/business-domain-types.md`; and committed-state tier-2 discharge, meaning proofs discharged from a standing declared fact against values persisted by earlier, separate operations. This is exactly the treatment reclassified bands already receive under owner decisions D2 (the band split) and D3 (atomicity relaxed). The compiler does **not** keep rejecting merely because the runtime is unbuilt. Because this is pre-release — there are no users, and every operation in `Evaluator.cs` throws `NotImplementedException` — a transient enforcement gap creates no live fault (D3). What keeps the deferred enforcement honest is the capture obligation of discipline 2, not a held compiler rejection.

**Definition of Done (redefined):** *compiler finished; runtime design fully captured in runtime canon; runtime coding handed off as an enumerated separate initiative.* The ten concrete criteria live in § 12.

---

## 1. Phase summary

Phases are ordered by **dependency and smallest-safe-increment**: each phase depends only on what earlier phases produce, and work lands in the smallest increments that are safe to ship independently. Owner decision D3 (atomicity relaxed) governs how strictly that ordering binds. Under D3, exactly one hard ordering constraint binds scheduling — a constraint whose violation would produce wrong results, as opposed to a mere preference. That constraint is: the fault-code registry must be honest before the Phase-2 checker asserts against it. Making the registry honest requires two Phase-1 slices: Slice 1.3, which reclassifies band violations per owner decision D2 (band split), and Slice 1.4, which repartitions the registry itself. The Phase-2 checker in question is the creation-exhaustiveness checker — the build-time check that enforces obligation-position totality, the property that the obligation-creation walk visits every catalogued child-expression position. (Phase 2's name in the table below pairs the property with the checker that enforces it — that is one checker, not two.) The reason the ordering is hard: that checker permanently encodes whichever fault-code routing the registry holds the first time it runs, so a dishonest registry would be baked in.

The plan's own text names other must-precede relationships. There are three. First, the grounding-research reconciliation: the research document that grounds the fault floor (`research/architecture/compiler/fault-floor-definition-2026-06-11.md`) was materially revised by owner decisions D1–D3. Two of those decisions were introduced above: D2, the band split, and D3, the atomicity relaxation. The third, owner decision D1, is the overflow park: decimal and temporal representability overflow — a computed value exceeding what the number or date types can represent — is accepted as a documented known limitation, with no prevention machinery built for it. Reconciling the research document against those decisions — Phase-0 work, Slice 0.4 — must land before any Phase-1-or-later slice begins. Second, the Phase-6 register merge must land before the Phase-7 conformance census. The register merge collapses into one source of truth the three separate diagnostic-completeness trackers: the emit-or-retire dispositions, the conformance suite's coverage tags, and the emission-ownership audit. The conformance census is the coverage census that Phase 7's conformance program runs in Slice 7.1, which takes the merged register as its input. Third, the honest registry must land before the Phase-6 liveness sweep — Slice 6.2's check that every fault code marked `[StaticallyPreventable]` has a live, actually-firing compile-time diagnostic behind it rather than a dead one (part of Phase 6's emit-or-retire discipline). Each of these three is already satisfied by the phase order as written — no extra scheduling machinery is needed — and none of them re-introduces the coordinated all-at-once merge coupling that D3 dissolved.

The table below gives each phase, what kind of work it is (using the four classification labels defined in the taxonomy immediately after the table), and what must be resolved before it can start.

| Phase | Name | Nature | Gating on |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | Design gates + read-only probes | CANON_DESIGN + read-only probes | owner conversations (GATE-B/C/D/E/F/G/H/J/K/L/N/O in Slice 0.2; GATE-I in Slice 0.3; GATE-M is NOT Phase 0 — it resolves at Phase-5 start) |
| 1 | Fault-registry honesty + band right-sizing (compiler half) | COMPILER_CODE + CANON_DESIGN | Phase 0 gate answers |
| 2 | Obligation-position totality + creation-exhaustiveness checker | COMPILER_CODE | **Phase 1 registry honest (HARD)** |
| 3 | False-Proved repairs + write-aware tier-2 (intra-chain) | COMPILER_CODE + CANON_DESIGN | Phase 1 registry; Phase 2 positions |
| 4 | Datatype-surface spec completeness (primitive/temporal/business) | COMPILER_CODE + CANON_DESIGN | Phase 0 GATE-J/K; Phase 3 proof machinery |
| 5 | Constructs spec completeness (collections/modifiers/expr/rules/states/binder/graph) | COMPILER_CODE + CANON_DESIGN | Phase 2/3 floor; GATE-M resolved at Phase-5 start |
| 6 | Diagnostic completeness + ownership analyzers | COMPILER_CODE + CANON_DESIGN | Phase 5 GATE-M resolution |
| 7 | Conformance program + API solidity + gate + handoff | COMPILER_CODE + CANON_DESIGN | all prior green |

Four notes on the table. First, the lettered gates (GATE-B through GATE-O) are the Phase-0 owner-decision gates — each is one open design or canon question the owner must rule on; most are resolved through owner conversations in Slice 0.2, GATE-I is resolved in Slice 0.3, and GATE-M is the sole exception whose resolution is deliberately deferred until Phase 5 starts, so it does not belong to Phase 0 at all. Three of the gates carry weight in the gating column itself, so their subjects belong here. GATE-J is the owner's ruling on two internal canon contradictions on the datatype surface. The first is the sqrt contradiction (spec §3.2 versus §3.7): spec §3.7 says an integer argument to sqrt is a type error, while §3.2's integer→number widening admits it — the canon disagrees with itself on whether sqrt accepts an integer. The second is the choice field-vs-field comparison contradiction: the canon holds two conflicting readings of how a choice-typed field compares against another field. That comparison today compiles with no diagnostics, but the clean compile is itself a soundness bug in the compiler — the comparison should be rejected, and it becomes a rejection once the reading is set. For both, the owner picks the canonical reading, and Phase 4's contested-reading slices wait on it. GATE-K is the owner's cross-unit policy ruling for the logarithmic units (dB, Np, B) — allow with what semantics, versus restrict; it too applies in Phase 4. GATE-M is the question of which single pipeline stage owns emission of PRE0141 — the unproved-assignment-qualifier-compatibility diagnostic — for bound expressions; it is expected to resolve as a side effect of Phase 5's Slice 5.1, which makes field-referencing bound modifiers participate in proof as rules (the second half of the locked relational-rules-and-bounds design), and that expected side effect is why its resolution is deliberately scheduled at Phase-5 start rather than in Phase 0.

Second, the one bolded entry — Phase 2 gating on "Phase 1 registry honest (HARD)" — is the single hard ordering constraint described above; every other entry in the column records a dependency to be resolved before that phase starts (a gate answered, or a prior phase's output in place), not a second hard ordering constraint — per D3, the hard orderings number exactly one.

Third, Phase 3's row name is built from proof-engine vocabulary that a cold reader meets here first, so it deserves unpacking the way Phase 2's row was unpacked above. A False-Proved hole is a proof-ledger entry that records a safety obligation as discharged when the fault is not actually ruled out; Phase 3 closes every known one. Tier-2 discharge means discharging a proof obligation from a standing declared fact — a rule or a modifier — rather than from a guard sitting next to the expression. Write-aware is the repair Phase 3 adds to tier-2 discharge: before a standing fact may discharge an obligation, every intra-chain (same-transaction) write to the constrained field that occurred earlier in the chain must be accounted for. Intra-chain names the scope of that repair. The repair applies only to the writes the compiler can actually see, meaning writes within a single event's action chain. Inside that chain, a stale discharge is genuine unprovability, and the compiler rejects it. The committed-state half is the other side of the same boundary: proofs discharged against values persisted by earlier, separate operations. Per the Slice 3.1 scope boundary, that committed-state half is admitted now, with its enforcement deferred to the runtime.

Fourth, Phase 7's row name contains the bare word "gate", and in that row it does not mean one of the lettered owner-decision gates from the first note. It names the runtime-ready gate — the plan's final readiness gate, which Phase 7 redefines so that no acceptance criterion asserts a runtime-execution outcome. "Handoff" in the same row names the runtime-coding handoff: the § 11 enumeration of the deferred runtime work, in which every deferred item's capture pointer — the runtime-canon section holding its design — is verified before owner sign-off.

### Classification taxonomy (every item gets exactly one)

Every item in this plan carries exactly one of the following four labels. The label determines where the work lives and how it is verified.

- **COMPILER_CODE** — compiler implementation work. It stays in this plan, and its results are verifiable at compile time (with the runtime still stubbed).
- **CANON_DESIGN** — canonical documentation and design work. It also stays in this plan; this label includes revisions to the runtime canon (`docs/runtime/*.md`) that capture decisions, even though no runtime code is written.
- **RUNTIME_CODE_DEFER** — work that requires the runtime to execute and therefore cannot be verified in this plan. It moves to the separate runtime initiative, and every such item MUST name its capture pointer — the specific runtime-canon section that holds its design — so nothing is deferred into silence.
- **PARKED** — a decision or piece of work deliberately not done, recorded with a reason as a documented known limitation. The parked set for this plan is the overflow families, the number-model redesign, the niche/flag layer, and the definition-evolution deploy work; each of these is stated in full, with a first-use explanation, in the next subsection.

### PARKED (not built; documented as known limitation)

The following items are deliberately not built in this plan. Each is recorded as a known limitation — none may be claimed done or prevented anywhere in the docs. The full list:

- Decimal-representability overflow, together with the whole representability obligation family it belongs to (this is owner decision D1's park).
- Temporal-arithmetic overflow, which is the same representability genus of fault applied to date/time computation (also under D1).
- The number-model redesign. The number model is the choice of how Precept represents numeric values; redesigning it (for example, moving to arbitrary-precision numerics) is the unmade decision the representability-overflow build was gated on, and it parks together with that build under D1.
- The maxplaces compile-time precision proof. `maxplaces N` is the field modifier that constrains a decimal or business-magnitude field to at most N digits after the decimal point; the parked proof would have established at compile time that no computed value can exceed that declared precision. It belongs to the same number-model class as the number-model redesign above and parks with it under owner decision D1.
- The post-runtime flag/niche layer — the layer of warning-severity diagnostics that can individually earn promotion to error once real runtime evidence exists.
- The definition-evolution deploy lane — the future work of rolling out an evolved (changed) precept definition, including any migration residue for data persisted under the earlier definition. Owner gate GATE-N confirms it out of floor scope as its own future runtime-plus-tooling decision.
- Absolute-measurement positions — a candidate piece of new language surface: the unit-system analog of the temporal "instant", meaning a measurement value that names an absolute position on a scale rather than a relative amount. Owner gate GATE-L carries it as a deferral with a recorded reason, not a work item.
- The two future-candidate lists. One is the proof-power list — ten candidate proof techniques the proof engine could adopt, ordered with Karr's affine-relation analysis as the first candidate ("Karr-first"). The other is the expressiveness list — five candidate language-surface extensions, maintained aggregates first. Both are carried as owner-gated stubs, not planned work.
- The ten graph modifiers recorded under finding F-LANG-SPEC-08, deferred to a post-runtime Compiler 1.1 — that is, to a follow-on compiler release planned for after the runtime ships.

### RUNTIME_CODE_DEFER (enumerated with capture pointers in § 11)

The following work requires the runtime to execute, so it is deferred rather than built here. Every item below has a row in § 11 (Runtime-Coding Handoff), and every row names the capture pointer — the runtime-canon section holding its design — so the deferral loses nothing. The deferred items:

- The governance/ingress sweep — the future runtime mechanism that evaluates every declared constraint at each commit and at each point where external values enter.
- Value-application — the evaluator computing concrete result values (for example, applying a cross-unit conversion factor so that 4.00 USD/ft multiplied by 36 in yields 12.00). The compiler's half of that split — deriving the result types — is built in this plan; the value computation is what defers.
- The four/five operations: Fire, Update, InspectFire, InspectUpdate, and Restore — the entity operations themselves. The count is written "four/five" because Restore's standing as a distinct fifth operation is what Phase 0 Slice 0.3 settles. The question turns on how persisted data is treated when it is loaded back into a live entity (re-hydrated) on restore. Under the trusted-hydration reading — the spec §0.7 position that persisted data is trusted as valid at the time it was persisted, is not re-validated at restore time, and is re-governed by the next operation that runs through the contract — Restore substantially dissolves rather than surviving as a full operation: what remains is re-governance by that next operation, plus a structural-consistency trap as backstop, meaning a runtime check kept in place that halts with a fault on any out-of-contract value.
- Committed-state tier-2 discharge enforcement — the runtime's half of the Slice 3.1 bargain, where the compiler admits proofs discharged against committed state now and the runtime later makes that sound by enforcing each constraint at every prior commit.
- Band governance enforcement — the runtime's half of owner decision D2, enforcing the reclassified non-proof-carrying bands at run time.
- Derived-value computations — the evaluator computing the concrete values of the cross-unit result families whose result types the compiler derives in this plan: the exchange-rate value from money divided by money; the compound-unit value from quantity divided by quantity; the inverse-division value, from dividing a quantity by a compound ratio quantity, where division inverts the ratio (for example, a value in each divided by a ratio in each-per-case yields case); and the time-denominator cancellation value, from multiplying a time-denominated price by a duration, where the time denominator cancels (for example, a price in USD per hours multiplied by an elapsed shift duration). The split is the same as for value-application above — the type derivation is compiler work built here; computing the value is what defers.

## 2. Decisions captured (apply — do not re-litigate)

Four owner decisions made this session reshape the prior plan. They are labeled D1 through D4. They are already applied throughout this document; every per-phase row assumes them. They are settled — a later section of the plan may cite them, but nothing may reopen them.

### D1 — OVERFLOW PARKED (GATE-A resolves as "park")

GATE-A is the Phase-0 owner-decision gate that asked what to do about representability overflow — the fault family where a computed value exceeds what the decimal or temporal types can represent. The owner resolved it as "park."

Concretely: decimal-representability overflow is an **accepted, documented, TEMPORARY known fault.** We do **not** build the representability-overflow obligation family. We build **no** bounds-checking machinery for it and **no** multi-field magnitude proof. The number-model redesign that prevention would depend on is itself parked. **Document overflow as a known limitation; never claim it prevented.** Temporal-arithmetic overflow is the same representability genus — a computed date or duration exceeding its type's range — and it parks together with the decimal case. The maxplaces compile-time precision proof (proving a computed value fits a declared decimal-places bound) belongs to the same number-model class of work, so it parks too.

- **Rationale:** the representability family was the single largest Phase-3 build, and it was the only fault-floor item gated on an undecided number model. Parking it collapses that risk and lets the rest of the floor ship now.
- **Alternatives rejected:** (a) build fixed-decimal numerics plus obligation-driven bounds plus prove-or-reject representability — too large, and gated on a number-model decision that has not been made; (b) arbitrary-precision numerics — that is a number-model redesign, and the redesign is parked.
- **Precedent:** `fault-floor-definition-2026-06-11.md` named GATE-A the highest-leverage relief valve; the owner closed it as park.
- **Tradeoff accepted:** the floor is **prove-or-reject EXCEPT** decimal/temporal representability overflow, which stands as a documented known fault that is never claimed prevented. This is an **honesty-about-approximation** carve-out — the guarantee is genuinely narrower here, not moved somewhere else (contrast D2 below, where prevention relocates rather than shrinks). Because this touches the product's stated guarantee, it is **owner-gated for `philosophy.md`** — surface the philosophy implication to the owner; do not auto-edit that document.

**Consequence on the registry (Phase 1):** the prior plan had a RETARGET action pointing the NumericOverflow fault code at a representability obligation that would be built; with D1, that obligation no longer exists, so the retarget has nothing to point at. Instead, keep `FaultCode.NumericOverflow` as a documented defense-in-depth known-fault trap — a runtime check that halts with a fault — with **no** live `[StaticallyPreventable]` guarantee behind it. Its `[StaticallyPreventable]` partner diagnostic is an honest, disclosed **gap**, not a claimed prevention. Do **not** add a `FaultCode.TemporalOverflow` member. One nearby item survives unchanged: the PRE0078 message split. PRE0078 is the band-check diagnostic whose current message text mixes two ideas; the split ensures the phrase "exceeded the representable range" stops attaching to what is actually a declared-bound check. That split still proceeds — it is compiler-pure and unrelated to the parked obligation.

**Compiler self-protection carve-out (NOT parked):** the compiler's own checked constant-fold — making sure a literal expression like `2e28 * 2e28` cannot crash the compilation pipeline itself — is compiler robustness. It is distinct from the parked runtime representability obligation, which is about governed data at runtime. The constant-fold hardening stays classified COMPILER_CODE and lands in Phase 4. Before scoping it, verify whether the compiler folds such literals at all.

### D2 — BAND RIGHT-SIZING SPLITS (compiler half in scope; enforcement deferred)

The prior plan carried one finding called the band-containment scale-back: the compiler was rejecting definitions over declared min/max, length, and count bounds (bands) it could not prove satisfied, even when nothing depended on those bounds for safety. D2 rules that this scale-back splits cleanly into a compiler half and a runtime half:

- **Compiler half (IN SCOPE, Phase 1):** reclassify the three fault codes `OutOfRange`, `LengthBound`, and `CountBound`, together with the PRE0078 band check, from fault/prove-or-reject down to a **compile-time flag/warning**. The warning fires only on a proven violation — a case where the compiler can actually demonstrate the bound is broken; merely-unprovable cases compile clean. Alongside the reclassification, trim the fault-code registry accordingly and amend the language spec. This half only **stops the compiler falsely rejecting valid precepts** — no runtime is needed to do it.
- **Runtime half (DEFER):** the governance sweep that **enforces** the reclassified bounds at each commit and each ingress point. The sweep must also enforce, at every prior commit, the constraints that compile-time proofs discharge against committed state. That per-commit checking is what makes committed-state tier-2 discharge sound — the runtime enforcement that justifies the compiler trusting previously persisted values. The design for this half is captured in the runtime canon and enumerated in § 11 (the Runtime-Coding Handoff); it is written down, not built.

- **Rationale:** the band lane was over-reach — the compiler was rejecting merely-unprovable intervals, not just proven violations. Downgrading to a proven-violation flag removes the false rejections without losing the genuine prevention signal.
- **Alternatives rejected:** keep prove-or-reject on bands (continues over-rejecting valid precepts); delete bands entirely (loses the genuine proven-violation signal that the flag preserves).
- **Precedent:** the scale-back findings SB-01..SB-08 in `fault-floor-definition-2026-06-11.md`.
- **Tradeoff accepted:** **prevention is relocated, not weakened** (Frank finding A6, from the adversarial review of the prior plan) — for non-proof-carrying bands and for values arriving at ingress, the future runtime's governance refusal is still atomic prevention; the write is refused whole. The enumerated impossibilities at `philosophy.md` L53 (divisor, overflow, empty-collection) are all **retained** floor families; bands are not in that list, so the philosophy's enumeration is untouched by this change. **Carve-out preserved:** the carries-proof set stays in the fault floor and keeps prove-or-reject. That set is: the mincount modifier's `count>0` fact, which feeds Strategy 2 (the proof engine's declaration-attribute discharge strategy, which discharges obligations from the subject's declared modifiers) on the branch of that strategy that discharges collection-access safety; and the facts contributed by sign modifiers and by declared rules, which likewise remain proof-carrying. One simplification falls out of D1: the prior analysis listed "bounds consumed by representability proofs" as a reason a band might carry proof, but with overflow parked there are no representability proofs to consume bounds, so that carries-proof reason **evaporates** — the non-proof-carrying exit is cleaner, with no representability reinstatement to hedge against. **Keep distinct from D1:** bands relocate their prevention to the runtime; overflow is genuinely relaxed — a disclosed limitation with no prevention anywhere.

### D3 — ATOMICITY RELAXED

The project is pre-release, has **no users**, and the runtime is a stub — so a transient window in which some check is enforced nowhere harms no one. Accordingly, **drop** the "atomic coordinated move so bounds are never enforced nowhere" machinery from the prior plan; it protects a running product we do not have. In particular, the prior plan carried a hard coupling it named "Slice-wiring-before-band-retirement": the runtime enforcement wiring — the runtime code that would enforce bounds at each commit and each ingress point — had to land before the compiler retired its band rejection, that is, before the band checks were reclassified from rejection down to the proven-violation warning (D2's compiler half). That coupling is **removed** — with the runtime stubbed, bounds are enforced nowhere regardless of ordering, so the no-enforcement window it guarded against is moot.

**KEEP the one scheduling-binding internal-correctness ordering.** Per D3, exactly one hard ordering constraint binds scheduling: the fault-code registry must be honest before the Phase-2 creation-exhaustiveness checker asserts against it. The creation-exhaustiveness checker is the build-time check, landing in Phase 2 (Slice 2.3), that fails the compiler's own build if obligation creation stops covering any catalogued expression position — the check that asserts the Phase-2 position-totality property. Making the registry honest requires two Phase-1 slices: the band reclassification (Slice 1.3) and the registry repartition (Slice 1.4) — the re-division of the fault-code registry that corrects which codes are listed as statically prevented and which diagnostic each fault family routes to. The reason the ordering is hard: a checker asserting against the pre-repartition registry — the registry as it stands before that re-division — would bake the dishonest routing into the structural guarantee. The plan's own text names three other must-precede relationships. First, the grounding-research reconciliation — Phase 0 Slice 0.4, which brings the grounding research document `fault-floor-definition-2026-06-11.md` up to date with this session's decisions before it feeds any agent — must precede any Phase-1-or-later slice. Second, the Phase-6 register merge — Slice 6.7, which collapses the three separate diagnostic-completeness trackers into one merged diagnostic register — must precede the Phase-7 conformance census, the Slice 7.1 census of the conformance suite's coverage that reads that one merged register as its input. Third, the honest registry must precede the Phase-6 liveness sweep — Slice 6.2, the sweep verifying that every fault code marked `[StaticallyPreventable]` has live prevention behind it, which asserts against the post-repartition registry. Each of those three is satisfied by the phase order as written, and none of them re-introduces atomic-merge coupling. Everywhere else, prefer simple incremental sequencing over all-or-nothing merges.

- **Rationale:** atomicity machinery exists to protect a live deployment; there isn't one.
- **Alternatives rejected:** keep the coordinated merge. That would couple a compiler change to a deferred runtime change that cannot land at all — the compiler half would be blocked indefinitely.
- **Precedent:** the MEMORY project note recording the solo-dev / pre-release / runtime-still-stub posture.
- **Tradeoff accepted:** a transient compile window exists in which the reclassified bands are enforced nowhere — harmless with no users and no runtime.

### D4 — Frank review fixes folded

Frank is the adversarial review that was run against the prior plan. D4 rules that its accepted findings are applied throughout this plan. In the review's own numbering, the B-prefixed findings were blocking and the A-prefixed findings were advisory (non-blocking). Each accepted finding and its application:

- **B1 (spec-amendment enumeration completeness):** GATE-F is the Phase-0 owner gate that enumerates the spec and philosophy canon amendments — a Tier-3 gate requiring owner approval, never bundled silently. Its amendment set is broader than the band change alone: it covers the language-spec amendments the band reclassification requires, plus the D1-driven overflow-honesty additions, plus the owner-gated `philosophy.md` surface. B1 requires that GATE-F's amendment set enumerate **every** sentence in the spec that states prove-or-reject behavior for bands, before the owner conversation happens — the owner must see the full list, not a sample. At minimum the set includes: §0.6 L256/L258 (the two Implementation-status tail paragraphs of §0.6) plus §0.7 L266; §0.6 item 6 (L208); Principle 11's "constraint range impossibility" fault class; **and** Principle 11 L112's clause reading "Runtime fault checks exist only as defensive redundancy," which needs an explicit ruling. On that last clause: a governance refusal is **not** a fault check — it is a structured refusal outcome, not a halt-with-fault — so the literal clause likely survives the band change unchanged. But the owner must be told about it, with the clause quoted verbatim, rather than having the question resolved silently.
- **B2 (DesugarsToRule consumer wording):** DesugarsToRule is the boolean flag in the modifier catalog declaring that a modifier desugars to a rule; it does not name which rule. Never describe it as having "ZERO consumers." The correct wording, everywhere it comes up: **"zero constraint-synthesis/enforcement consumers; 2 cosmetic/display consumers exist** — GrammarGen TextMate color lane (`GrammarGen/Program.cs:173`) and the MCP formatter (`CatalogFormatters.cs:253+`). No pipeline stage synthesizes it into a TypedRule/TypedEnsure."
- **B3 (28-vs-32 provenance):** the prior plan's counts of triaged source documents disagreed (28 in one place, 32 in another). Reconcile both to the single statement: **"43 mined obligations recovered from the triage of 32 prior docs (28 superseded + 4 archived)."** ("Mined" means recovered by triaging the prior working documents.) Two follow-up checks: confirm that the obligations transferred out of the 4 archived docs are actually present in that register of 43 obligations recovered from triaging 32 prior working documents (Coverage Ledger §E); and reconcile any stated FaultCode member-count against the live `FaultCode.cs` source.
- **A1 (status field):** the plan's front-matter status is `Draft plan — 2026-06-16`, not `Active` — the plan has not been executed, and the status field must say so.
- **A6 (prevention relocated not weakened):** make the band argument — that D2 relocates prevention to future runtime governance rather than removing it — explicit in the GATE-F framing and in the runtime-handoff framing. Keep it **distinct** from D1's overflow, which is genuinely relaxed (a parked known fault), not relocated.
- **A7 (Phase-3 risk):** the representability family was the highest hidden risk in Phase 3's build. D1 **dissolves** that risk by parking the family. Do not carry it forward as a Phase-3 build risk.

## 3. Phase 0 — Design gates + read-only probes (CURRENT)

**Goal:** Settle every design question that a downstream compiler slice depends on, and **capture each answer in the canonical docs**, before any code lands. Three workstreams converge in this phase. The first is a set of factual **read-only probes**: each probe runs `precept_compile` and records the diagnostics it returns — never runtime execution — so that the behavior the later phases will reclassify is documented as observed fact rather than asserted from memory. The second is the set of **owner-decision gates**: the open language and diagnostic-semantics questions, each carried as a lettered gate — the canonical Phase-0 roster is enumerated in Slices 0.2 and 0.3 below and restated at the Phase 0 exit — and each surfaced to the owner conversationally, one at a time, with the options framed neutrally, per the Pre-Design Owner Consultation gate in CLAUDE.md. The third is the set of **runtime-canon design reconciliations**: every runtime contract the compiler's behavior forces is written into `docs/runtime/*.md`, so that the deferred runtime build inherits a complete specification instead of rediscovering these contracts later. On any gate that touches language surface, no agent is spawned, no `/design` document is written, and no readiness-plan row is expanded until the owner conversation reaches **alignment** — alignment on the shape, which is a different and stronger thing than a yes/no approval. Phase 0 builds **nothing**. Its outputs are dispositions, owner directions, and runtime-canon notes. The plan itself is promoted from `Draft plan` status to `Locked` only when this phase clears.

**Dependencies:** Nothing upstream blocks this phase; owner availability is the only constraint. One internal obligation does gate the *next* phase: the grounding research document `research/architecture/compiler/fault-floor-definition-2026-06-11.md` must be reconciled — following the § A checklist in the design-revision companion, `compiler-readiness-plan-2026-06-16-design-revision-notes.md` (that companion's § A is a checklist of edits to the research doc; it is a different thing from the Coverage Ledger's §A input register of floor gaps later in this plan, which happens to share the letter) — **before** that research doc grounds any Phase-1-or-later slice. That reconciliation is itself a Phase-0 work item (Slice 0.4). It gates Phase 1 onward, not Phase 0 itself.

**Effort:** S (3–6 days). The phase is almost entirely conversation, read-only probes, and design reconciliation. The only mechanical surfaces are the probe runs themselves and the authoring of the runtime-canon notes.

**Boundary classification:** The whole phase is **CANON_DESIGN plus read-only probes**. It contains **zero COMPILER_CODE** and **zero RUNTIME_CODE**. The phase *authorizes and specifies* the COMPILER_CODE that Phases 1 and later will land, and it *captures* the RUNTIME_CODE_DEFER design — but it lands no executable change of either kind. The one compiler-completable artifact the phase touches, the probe runs, is read-only diagnostics output, not an edit to anything that builds. Every exit criterion therefore takes the form "disposition recorded / owner direction captured / runtime-canon note written" — each is verifiable by inspecting the recorded artifact, and none asserts a runtime-execution outcome.

---

### Slice 0.1 — Read-before-asserting probes (read-only `precept_compile`; record factual disposition; build nothing)

- **Work:** Run three read-only probes, and record each one as a **disposition row containing the verbatim probe input and the verbatim output**. This is the read-before-asserting discipline: the factual behavior is recorded from observation, never asserted from memory. All three probes are `precept_compile` diagnostics with the runtime still stubbed — **no runtime execution happens, no fault code is added, and no proof obligation is built.**
  - **(a) Temporal-arithmetic overflow probe.** Compile an expression that adds or subtracts an out-of-range `period` to a `date` or `datetime` — this exercises the NodaTime arithmetic boundary — and record whatever diagnostic or behavior the compiler factually produces. The underlying obligation is **PARKED together with decimal overflow** under owner decision D1, the decision that parks both decimal and temporal representability overflow ("same representability genus"). Because D1 is settled, this probe is now an **evidence-gathering input to the known-limitation honesty note**, not scoping work for a build. Its output feeds the overflow honesty documentation written in Phase 1 — the `evaluator.md §10` carve-out, the Principle-11 softening, and the §0.7 carve-out, per the design-revision companion § A items 6 and 11(2). **Do not let this probe re-inject a `FaultCode.TemporalOverflow` or a representability obligation — D1 parks both.** The probe documents the limitation; it does not scope a build.
  - **(b) omit × global-rule join adjudication.** Determine which of two categories the interaction between `omit` and global rules belongs to. It could be **floor-shaped** — a presence-lane join in obligation creation; if the interaction is a join in that lane, the position walk for it is queued into Phase 2 Slice 2.3, the slice that builds the creation-exhaustiveness checker. Or it could be **flag-shaped** — a matter of PRE0082 hook-guard coverage. PRE0082 is `UnsatisfiableGuard`, the proof engine's satisfiability-scan warning for a guard that can never be true; the flag-shaped reading makes the omit interaction a question of extending that coverage to hook guards. That coverage extension belongs to the post-runtime flag layer and is therefore OUT of compiler scope. **If the adjudication moves it to the flag layer, surface that reclassification explicitly — no silent move** — because a flag-layer move removes the item from the fault floor, and removals from the floor must be visible. This disposition decides exactly one downstream conditional: Slice 2.3's checker folds the omit-join in **only if** this probe found it floor-shaped.
  - **(c) PRE0101 false-emptiness confirmation.** Confirm the currently observed collapse where the `CollectionEmptyOnMutation` empty-collection message is mis-attached to the duplicate-key (PRE0101 / KeyUniquenessGuard) path. This collapse is verified present at `diagnostic-system.md:590/624` — `CollectionEmptyOnMutation = 10`, whose message "Dequeue/pop called on empty collection" is mis-attached to the duplicate-key site. Alongside confirming the collapse, **frame** the semantics question it raises — whether a keyed add means "require the key to be absent" or "put/overwrite" — because that question feeds GATE-D in Slice 0.2. Note the division of labor: the false-emptiness *fix* is COMPILER_CODE and lands in Phase 1 Slice 1.4 regardless of how GATE-D resolves; this probe only confirms the collapse and frames the routing decision.
- **Exit criteria:** Three disposition rows are recorded, each with verbatim probe input and output. The temporal-overflow probe's behavior is captured for use in the limitation documentation. The omit-join is classified as floor-shaped or flag-shaped, and if flag-shaped, the reclassification is surfaced explicitly. The PRE0101 collapse is confirmed against HEAD and the duplicate-key semantics question is framed for GATE-D. **No fault code is added; no obligation is built; no `.precept` corpus file is edited.** All of this is verifiable by inspecting the three recorded rows — none of them asserts a runtime outcome.
- **Doc-touch:** The dispositions are recorded in the PR body, which is the right home because probe results are transient project state, not canon. The temporal probe result is queued as input into the overflow known-limitation note that Phase 1 Slice 1.5 authors; that note's target documents are `docs/language/precept-language-spec.md` (the §0.7 and Principle 11 overflow softening), `docs/runtime/evaluator.md §10` (the carve-out), and `docs/runtime/fault-system.md` (NumericOverflow recorded as known-not-prevented). No canonical doc is edited *in this slice* — the probe is input to a later canon edit.
- **Gate:** None. The slice is read-only and touches no language surface. Two of its outputs feed later decision points, but neither is itself a gate: the omit-join disposition from probe (b) **feeds the Phase-2 conditional fold** (Slice 2.3 folds the omit-join in only if (b) adjudicated it floor-shaped), and the PRE0101 disposition from probe (c) **feeds** GATE-D in Slice 0.2. Both are factual probes, not gates.

---

### Slice 0.2 — Language / diagnostic-semantics gates (surface conversationally, settle, capture)

- **Work:** Walk the owner through each open gate **in plain text, one gate at a time, neutrally framed**. No AskUserQuestion modals; the fault-floor research supplies recommendations, and the owner decides. For each gate, the conversation follows the consultation pattern: name the gap, cite the canonical-doc area that was checked, sketch the shape under consideration as a conversation-opener rather than a settled proposal, and capture either the owner's direction or an explicit "defer to Phase N start" with the reason recorded. Every gate is decided by **design** — never by running the runtime. The gates:
  - **GATE-B — write-aware tier-2 discharge shape.** This is the design conversation for Phase 3 Slice 3.1, the slice that makes tier-2 discharge (discharging a proof obligation from a standing declared rule or modifier rather than from an adjacent guard) aware of writes that may invalidate the standing fact. Two candidate implementation shapes exist: (i) attach preservation obligations to writes into proof-carrying constraints, versus (ii) apply staleness-invalidation to the declaration/rule fact and then re-establish it forward from the written expression's interval. If the gate is unresolved when the slice starts, the conservative default is **shape (ii), the reuse path**. The reasoning: the collection lane already implements exactly that staleness discipline, whereas shape (i) would re-aim the PRE0078 band mechanism — the very mechanism Phase 1 reclassifies under owner decision D2 (the band split) — making (i) the weaker fit after D2. The chosen discharge shape is captured in `docs/compiler/proof-engine.md` and applied in Phase 3 Slice 3.1.
  - **GATE-C — qualifier assignment-lane taxonomy, LABEL only.** The behavior here is **settled**: keep both rejections and build the qualifier-source obligation in Phase 3 Slice 3.2. The only open question is the LABEL — whether these cases carry the `QualifierMismatch` identity or a named third category for type-identity violations. The build proceeds under either label and **never blocks** on this gate. The chosen label is recorded in `diagnostic-system.md` (the qualifier routing) and `fault-system.md` (the fault-code identity).
  - **GATE-D — PRE0101 duplicate-key semantics.** Two readings of a keyed add: (i) a keyed add requires the key to be absent, so a duplicate key produces a `DuplicateKey` fault and the case stays in the fault lane; versus (ii) a keyed add means put/overwrite, so duplicate-key uniqueness is a **rule**, which moves the case into governance — it exits the fault lane, and its enforcement defers to the runtime. The false-emptiness collapse that probe 0.1c confirms is wrong under EITHER reading and is therefore **fixed regardless**, as COMPILER_CODE in Phase 1 Slice 1.4; only the routing of `DuplicateKey` is gated here. The decision lands in `diagnostic-system.md` (PRE0101 routing) and in `collection-types.md` plus the spec (the require-absence-versus-put semantics). Under reading (ii), the uniqueness enforcement becomes RUNTIME_CODE_DEFER, with its capture pointer at `evaluator.md §7.6` (governance refusal).
  - **GATE-E — mincount-at-birth Create semantics.** When a collection field declares a minimum count, what does Create mean? Three candidate rulings: (i) a birth exemption — the minimum does not apply at creation; (ii) a deferred check that fires at first mutation; or (iii) a defined refusal when every possible Create fails, paired with a compile-time flag. The count scale-back — the piece of owner decision D2, the band split, that reclassifies the count-bound family from a prove-or-reject fault to a proven-violation compile-time flag — forces this ruling so that the reclassification routes correctly. The classification split matters: **the compile-side ledger-honesty fix — correcting the proof ledger's at-birth seed so it no longer reports `Proved` at Create for a `mincount`-bounded field that is empty at birth — and the every-Create-fails definition-incoherence flag are COMPILER_CODE, landing in Phase 3 Slice 3.3; the runtime enforcement of refusal-at-Create (under rulings (i) or (ii)) is RUNTIME_CODE_DEFER.** One thing does not change under any ruling: mincount **retains** its proof-carrying `count > 0` discharge arm under Strategy 2, Declaration Attribute Proof — the proof-engine strategy that discharges an obligation from the subject's declared modifiers and other declaration-site metadata, which is exactly how a declared `mincount` discharges `count > 0`. That discharge arm is compiler work in Phases 1 and 3. The ruling lands in `evaluator.md §7.1` (the Create verdict space plus the Create-time enforcement capture) and the spec's cardinality canon.
  - **GATE-F — spec/philosophy canon-amendment ENUMERATION.** This gate is Tier-3 under the owner-consultation tiers — it amends locked spec text, so it requires owner approval and is never bundled silently into other edits. It applies **Frank finding B1** (a factual-accuracy fix from the adversarial plan review): **the enumeration of amendment sites must be COMPLETE before the owner conversation happens.** Every sentence in the spec that states band prove-or-reject is named here, verbatim, all verified against HEAD this pass:
    - **§0.7 L266** — "no result outside a declared bound" (verbatim: *"no division by zero, no overflow, no empty-collection access, no result outside a declared bound"*).
    - **§0.6 L256** (the Implementation-status tail) — the relational/divisor "never compiles a false 'safe'" clause. Rule-sourced relations participate in proof, and the count-cardinality language rides this section.
    - **§0.6 L258** (the Implementation-status tail) — the count-cardinality prove-or-reject sentence (verbatim: *"count is 'no result outside a declared bound', discharged by an author guard, never deferred to a runtime check"* — this is the sentence behind `CountBoundViolation`/PRE0136).
    - **§0.6 item 6 (L208)** — "Assignment range impossibility… is a compile-time error". This softens to **proven-violation-only**: a warning that fires only when the compiler can prove the violation, i.e. a flag, not prove-or-reject.
    - **Principle 11 — the "constraint range impossibility" fault class** (L112 verbatim: *"type mismatch, division by zero, overflow, empty collection access, constraint range impossibility"*). Two members of this list need softening: the band member ("constraint range impossibility") and the overflow member.
    - **Principle 11 L112, the "defensive redundancy" clause** (verbatim: *"Runtime fault checks exist only as defensive redundancy, never as the primary enforcement mechanism"*). Quote this clause to the owner and obtain an **explicit ruling**. The likely resolution: a governance refusal is **not** a "fault check," so the literal clause probably **survives** the band relocation — this is Frank finding A6's framing, "relocated, not weakened." But the owner must see the exact words and rule on them, because **this is the clause most at risk of being silently contradicted.**
    - **The D1 overflow honesty addition.** The overflow and representability claims in Principle 11 (L112, "overflow") and §0.7 (L266, "no overflow") now need softening to a **disclosed known limitation** — the statement becomes "decimal/temporal representability overflow is an accepted, NOT-prevented, documented known fault." This addition to the GATE-F set is driven by D1. It is **honesty-about-approximation**, and it is a different kind of change from the band relocation: bands relocate their prevention to future runtime governance, while overflow prevention is genuinely relaxed.
    - **`philosophy.md` is OWNER-GATED and never auto-edited.** Its reconciliation of absolute claims does not ride this amendment set; it rides the separate guarantee-statement promotion — the owner-authorized pass that lifts the revised core-guarantee wording into `philosophy.md` as its own deliberate change, never bundled into an implementation PR. In this slice: surface the gap to the owner — the core-guarantee category changed, because overflow is now a disclosed limitation and bands relocate to runtime governance per Frank A6 — supply the A6 framing for that conversation, and then **wait**. Do not edit `philosophy.md`.
  - **GATE-G — min-bound discharge vacuity.** Declared minimum bounds currently discharge nothing, making the discharge tier vacuous for them. Two resolutions: (i) make `DeclarationValue` satisfactions resolvable in the proof engine, which is COMPILER_CODE landing in Phase 3 Slice 3.4; or (ii) amend the §0.7 discharge-tier sentence, which folds into GATE-F and lands as CANON_DESIGN in Phase 1 Slice 1.5. **With overflow parked under D1, option (i)'s strongest motivation — feeding representability proofs — evaporates**, so the spec-narrowing path (ii) is the cleaner default disposition. Accounting note: this is a **single register entry — CANON_DESIGN with a conditional COMPILER_CODE tail**. That single-entry treatment reconciles the prior triple-count, where the same question appeared three times under three different row identities: as P0-GATE-G, its entry in this phase's gate roster; as FG-14, row 14 of the floor-gaps input register (Coverage Ledger §A); and as a row in the grounding research doc's own FF-prefixed re-pass (the doc-revision rows Slice 0.4 labels as revision, not work). It is one decision with a conditional in-engine tail, not three work items.
  - **GATE-H — three-category taxonomy adoption.** Confirm the taxonomy under which every diagnostic belongs to exactly one of three categories. Option (i) is the recommended shape, and **both independent derivations converged on it**: the grounding fault-floor research (`research/architecture/compiler/fault-floor-definition-2026-06-11.md`) ran two independent floor derivations — two copies of the derivation performed separately, so that convergence between them counts as evidence — and both arrived at the same three categories. Category 1 is the fault floor (prove-or-reject). Category 2 is definition incoherence — definitions wrong regardless of data, such as PRE0079-on-default (the `OutOfRange` diagnostic firing where a field's declared default value violates the field's own declared bound), PRE0164 (`DefaultViolatesRule`: an unguarded global rule provably violated by the field default values), and PRE0115 (`UnsatisfiableInitialState`: the initial state's `ensure` provably violated by those same defaults); definition incoherence is always Error severity, covers cases like "every Create fails," and belongs to neither the fault lane nor the flag layer. Category 3 is the flag layer (post-runtime, warning-default, with each diagnostic individually able to earn promotion to error on real runtime evidence). Option (ii) — folding definition incoherence into the flag layer — is **rejected, because it contradicts the flag layer's own promotion criterion**: a flag-layer diagnostic starts as a warning and earns promotion to error only on real runtime evidence, while incoherence is wrong by construction and has nothing to earn promotion from. The task at this gate is to confirm (i). The consequent compile-time identity reframe of PRE0079-on-default, PRE0164, and PRE0115 is COMPILER_CODE landing in Phase 1 Slice 1.4. **Sub-note driven by D1:** category 1, the fault floor, now needs an "accepted known faults" sub-section to hold the parked overflow class. Targets: spec §0.6/§0.7, `diagnostic-system.md`, and `fault-system.md`.
  - **GATE-J — sqrt §3.2-vs-§3.7 and choice field-vs-field internal-canon contradictions.** Two places where the spec contradicts itself internally: the sqrt treatment differs between §3.2 and §3.7, and the choice-type field-vs-field comparison has a false-clean — a case where the compiler reports the definition clean when it should reject it, exactly the false "safe" the spec's §0.6 "never compiles a false 'safe'" clause forbids. The owner picks the canonical reading; the implementation follows it. The choice field-vs-field false-clean is a soundness-grade compiler bug that gets fixed once the reading is set. That fix rides the Phase 4 tail: the contested-reading slices at the end of Phase 4, Slices 4.6 and 4.10, which are the only Phase-4 slices this owner reconciliation blocks. Separately, Phase 3 Slice 3.3 routes choice-default membership checking to the type checker. Classification: CANON_DESIGN with a compiler tail.
  - **GATE-K — log-unit (dB/Np/B) cross-unit policy.** The question is whether cross-unit operations on logarithmic units are allowed (and if allowed, with what semantics) or restricted. This is **Tier-2 language surface, so it requires owner consultation before any `/design` invocation.** An interim floor already holds — `ScaleIsRational=false` blocks the unsound cases — so resolving this gate is unblocking work, not urgent work. Application lands in Phase 4. Surface the proposal shape conversationally; **do not propose a specific syntax inline** — route to `/design` only after alignment.
  - **GATE-L — absolute-measurement positions.** The question of whether to build the "instant"-analog for units — an absolute-position counterpart to relative quantities — or to continue deferring it. Recommended disposition: **DEFER**, because it is new language surface. When it is eventually taken up, it gets the full Tier-2 consultation plus a `/design` pass. It is carried as a **deferral with a recorded reason**, not as a Phase-4 work item.
  - **GATE-N — definition-evolution deploy lane.** How evolved definitions are deployed against previously persisted entities. Disposition: **CONFIRM OUT** of the fault-floor scope — it is its own future runtime-plus-tooling decision. This is confirm-not-build; the reason is recorded, namely that its eventual build is a separate initiative. The confirmation is re-checked at the Phase-7 gate.
  - **GATE-O — integer-conversion-overflow / non-finite (∞/NaN) fault-code disposition.** Slice 4.9 will capture new fault codes for integer-conversion overflow and non-finite values into `fault-system.md`. But DoD-9 — the ninth Definition-of-Done criterion — carries a §10 contract permitting a post-clean-compile evaluator fault ONLY for the parked decimal/temporal representability family. The new codes today sit in no valid category: they are not inside that park, not covered by any compile-time obligation, and not explicitly parked in their own right. The owner must give one of three dispositions: (a) fold them inside the parked representability family, which widens D1's stated scope; (b) declare them a new fault lane that is owed compile-time prevention; or (c) give them their own explicit park. Targets: `docs/runtime/fault-system.md` and `evaluator.md §10`. Slice 4.9 applies whichever answer the owner gives, and DoD-9's contract wording follows it.
- **Exit criteria:** Every gate carries either a **recorded owner direction** or an explicit "defer to Phase N start" with a reason. The **GATE-F amendment set is enumerated complete per Frank B1** — every §0.7, §0.6, and Principle-11 site listed above is named with its verbatim text — and is acknowledged as owner-gated and queued for Phase 1 Slice 1.5. No canon is edited in this slice: amendments are only *approved* here and *applied* in Phase 1. GATE-A is recorded as **resolved (park)** — it appears in the roster only to document that resolution, since D1 already settled it. The **overflow philosophy gap is surfaced** to the owner, and the `philosophy.md` reconciliation is recorded as owner-gated and never auto-edited. GATE-B, C, D, E, G, H, J, K, L, N, and O each carry a disposition. The **consultation evidence is visible** in the conversation record — what canonical area was checked, what was found there, and what the owner authorized — so a reviewer can verify it after the fact by grepping the conversation. **No canon edit, no agent spawn, and no `/design` write happens on any language-surface gate (GATE-K, GATE-L) until alignment is reached.** All of this is verifiable by inspecting the recorded gate dispositions.
