## Sequencing red-team

The prior plan carried a red-team section built around an *atomic coordinated move* — an all-or-nothing merge in which the compiler-side band reclassification and the runtime-side enforcement would land together, so that reclassified bounds were never enforced nowhere. Owner decision D3 (atomicity relaxed) retired that machinery: there is no running product to protect from a no-bounds-enforced window, so the all-or-nothing merge apparatus is gone. Retiring the machinery does **not** retire the obligation to stress-test the ordering — it changes what the stress test must prove. This section adversarially probes the 8-phase ordering against four questions:

1. Does the single retained hard dependency actually hold, and is it the *only* one?
2. Is dropping the atomic coordinated move genuinely safe, given the stubbed runtime and the absence of users?
3. Is there a missed cross-phase dependency that the incremental sequencing would trip over?
4. Does any *retained* (non-deferred) slice assert a runtime-**execution** exit criterion — which would be a boundary violation — and is the **enforcement** of every admitted fault-safe-but-runtime-dependent operation captured in §11 (Runtime-Coding Handoff) with a capture pointer, so that nothing suffers silent loss?

Each probe states the stress, the evidence, and a verdict.

### Ground-truth anchors used by every probe

- **Runtime is a pure stub (verified).** In `src/Precept/Runtime/Evaluator.cs`, all five entry points — `Fire` (`:80`), `Update` (`:99`), `InspectFire` (`:121`), `InspectUpdate` (`:132`), and `Restore` (`:155`) — `throw new NotImplementedException()`, and each is tagged "once the executable model is designed." No constraint or governance sweep exists, and the executable model is undesigned. The consequence: any slice whose exit criterion needs the runtime to execute cannot be a compiler-completable slice.
- **Bijective core (verified).** The bijective core — the one-to-one pairing of statically-prevented fault codes with the compile-time diagnostics that prevent them — lives at `StaticallyPreventableMap.cs:28-37` and lists `DivisionByZero, SqrtOfNegative, UnguardedCollectionAccess, UnguardedCollectionMutation, NumericOverflow, LengthBoundViolation, CountBoundViolation`. `OutOfRange`/`IntervalContainment` is **absent** from that list. This confirms finding A2 from Frank — the adversarial plan review whose accepted findings owner decision D4 folds in: removing OutOfRange from the bijective core is a no-op, because it was never there; only the Length and Count rows are real removals.
- **No users / pre-release (the precondition D3 rests on).** The entire corpus that compiles against this compiler is `samples/` plus test fixtures, all repaired in-tree (Phase 2 Slice 2.4, the corpus-repair slice). There is no external consumer that a transient compile window could harm.

---

### Stress 1 — Does the single hard dependency (registry honest BEFORE the Phase-2 position checker) actually hold, and is it really the only one?

**The claim under test (D3).** Exactly one cross-phase ordering is load-bearing: Phase 1 Slice 1.4 — the slice that trims and repartitions the fault-code registry — must land before Phase 2's obligation-position-totality work and its creation-exhaustiveness checker read that registry. Everything else sequences incrementally, with no atomic coupling anywhere.

**Why the dependency is real, not decorative.** The Phase-2 checker proves a structural property: the set of declared child `TypedExpression` fields of each subtype, minus the set of positions the obligation-creation walk recurses into, must be empty. The walk it gates routes faults through the registry's codes. If the checker and the closed walk asserted against the *pre*-repartition registry, three concrete dishonesties would be baked into the structural guarantee:

- (a) `LengthBoundViolation` and `CountBoundViolation` would still carry `[StaticallyPreventable]` — the attribute claiming a compile-time diagnostic prevents the fault. The checker would then assert position-totality for obligation families that owner decision D2 (the band split) reclassified to compile-time flags. In other words, the checker would demand walk-coverage for a family that is no longer in the fault floor.
- (b) The `_ => DivisionByZero` fallback (`ProofEngine.Diagnostics.cs:488-491`) would still be live. A newly-walked position could then emit a divisor message for a presence, qualifier, or dimensional fault. The "every position is covered" proof would hold — but the positions would be *covered with the wrong code*.
- (c) The `IndexOutOfBounds` and `KeyNotFound` rows, and the `UnexpectedNull` bijective row, would not yet be added. Three diagnostics depend on those rows: PRE0100, the index-bounds diagnostic that should route through `IndexOutOfBounds`; PRE0099, the key-presence diagnostic that should route through `KeyNotFound`; and PRE0116, the presence diagnostic that should route through the `UnexpectedNull` bijective row. Without those rows, each would keep reporting the wrong failure at exactly the positions the walk newly closes: the index and key faults would keep producing false "collection was empty" messages, and the presence fault would keep falling through the backstop to a divisor message.

The checker permanently locks in whichever fault-code routing the registry holds the first time it runs, so the registry must be corrected before the checker lands. The ordering is therefore causal, not stylistic.

**Is it the *only* hard ordering? — adversarial search for a second.** Walking every other inter-phase edge, looking for a *hard* coupling — one whose violation would break correctness rather than merely offend tidiness:

- **Phase 0 → all.** Phase 0 is a design-gate and read-only probe phase. It produces routing answers and captured runtime canon, but no code. Downstream phases *consume* gate answers — for example, Phase 1 Slice 1.1 stages them — but a gate left as "defer to Phase N start" is an explicit soft handoff, not a hard build-order coupling. Each such gate carries a conservative default posture that lets the build proceed under either resolution. The general default is prove-or-reject: the obligation-creation walk covers every position, and the unprovable is rejected. For GATE-C, the qualifier-taxonomy gate, the default is the settled behavior itself: both rejections in the qualifier assignment lane are kept. Under GATE-C only the diagnostic's label is open, and the build stays label-agnostic: the obligation's creation, discharge, and reject behavior is identical whichever fault-code label GATE-C picks — the existing `QualifierMismatch` identity, or a named third category for type-identity violations. That candidate third category, if chosen, is a fault-code identity; despite the similar wording, it is unrelated to the three-category taxonomy of fault floor, definition incoherence, and flag layer. **Soft, not hard — with one exception.** GATE-F (approved by the owner in Phase 0 Slice 0.2, applied in Slice 1.5, the spec-amendments slice) is a Tier-3 owner-approved spec amendment — a change conflicting with locked spec text, requiring the heaviest consultation depth — and it has **no** conservative default. It cannot land without recorded owner approval, so the rationale "gates are soft because a conservative default exists" does not cover it. The phase order still satisfies it, because Phase 0 precedes Slice 1.5. But it is an owner-approval hard point, not a soft handoff.
- **Slice 0.4 → every Phase-1+ slice (the grounding-research reconciliation).** Slice 0.4 reconciles the stale grounding-research document before any Phase-1+ slice feeds it to an agent. The plan itself labels this a hard internal ordering, and this survey previously omitted it. It is hard by the plan's own text. It is also satisfied by the phase order as written, since Phase 0 precedes all Phase-1+ work. **Hard, satisfied by phase order.**
- **Slice 1.4 → the Phase-6 liveness sweep (Slice 6.2).** Slice 6.2 sweeps every `[StaticallyPreventable]` attribute to confirm its partner diagnostic actually fires. The Phase-6 dependency text says that running this sweep before the registry is honest "would chase codes mid-flight." That is the same failure family as the Phase-2 checker edge — an asserter reading over the registry — with the same producer (Slice 1.4). The edge is softened by three facts: the sweep is attribute-derived, it self-corrects on re-run, and five phases of slack separate the producer from the consumer. But by this red-team's own hardness criterion, it is a second instance of the family. **Hard-family, satisfied by phase order.**
- **Slice 6.7 → Slice 7.1 (register merge before the conformance census).** Slice 6.7 produces the merged diagnostic register — the single output tracker that collapses the three separate diagnostic-completeness trackers into one source of truth. Slice 7.1 is the Phase-7 conformance census that reads it. The Phase-7 header names this ordering hard in so many words, and Slice 7.1's exit criterion — read the one merged register, do not stand up a second tracker — is unsatisfiable before 6.7 exists. **Hard, satisfied by phase order.**
- **Phase 1 → Phase 3.** Phase 3's write-aware tier-2 discharge work and its qualifier-source obligations reuse the obligation-creation and discharge machinery, and they reuse the reclassified registry. But Phase 3 *adds* obligations and *closes* False-Proved holes against whatever registry exists at the time; it does not bake a structural proof over the registry the way Phase 2 does. If Phase 1's reclassification were incomplete when Phase 3 ran, Phase 3 would over-reject — the safe direction — rather than falsely admit. **Soft (safe-direction), not hard.**
- **Phase 2 → Phase 3.** Phase 3's conservative posture — unprovable means reject — is independent of position-totality. Phase 2 closing the 10 fail-open positions only *adds* obligations that Phase 3 must then discharge or reject. Running Phase 3 first would mean some positions still fail open — positions the obligation-creation walk never visits, so a fault there silently collects no obligation — but that is the *pre-existing* unsound state, not a new corruption, and Phase 2 later closes it. The plan orders 2 before 3 for cleanliness: do not repair discharge logic over holes you are about to close. But the ordering is **preference, not correctness** — neither order creates a false-clean, that is, a spuriously clean compile result that later work would then build on as if it were sound.
- **Phase 3 → Phases 4/5.** The phase summary gates Phase 4 on "Phase 3 proof machinery" — qualifier and dimensional discharge plus write-awareness — and gates Phase 5 on the "Phase 2/3 floor." This is the "breadth after the floor holds" preference: new datatype and construct surface should land against a floor that is right-sized, position-total, and sound, so a new construct does not inherit a fail-open position or a band over-rejection. But if a Phase-4 type-derivation fix landed before Phase 3, the worst case is that a not-yet-closed False-Proved hole coexists with the new surface. Again, that is the *pre-existing* unsound state, not a new corruption. **Soft (quality-ordering), not hard.**
- **Phase 1 Slice 1.4 pow-exponent misroute fix ↔ Phase 4 pow-exponent message work.** This is a "land once" coordination, not an ordering. The routing fix is executed in Slice 1.4, and Phase 4 cross-references it rather than re-implementing it. The only failure mode is *duplication*, and the merge-once discipline catches that — it is not a build-order hazard.
- **Phase 5 2c-ii synthesis ↔ Phase 1 Slice 1.2 DesugarsToRule synthesis (the unconsumed-artifact coupling).** Here 2c-ii is the second half of the locked relational-rules-and-bounds design — making field-referencing bound modifiers participate in proof — and Slice 1.2 is the slice that turns DesugarsToRule catalog metadata into synthesized typed rules. This pairing is the subtlest candidate for a hidden hard edge. See Stress 3, where it resolves to a *decision* coupling — land-ahead versus co-defer — not a build-order one.

**Verdict — HOLDS as the one scheduling-binding ordering; the literal "only hard ordering" count is corrected.** The registry-honest-before-checker dependency is causal, because the checker permanently locks in whatever routing the live registry holds when it first runs. Per D3, exactly one hard ordering constraint binds scheduling: the fault registry must be honest — which requires both the band reclassification (Slice 1.3) and the registry repartition (Slice 1.4) — before the Phase-2 position-totality checker asserts against it. The plan's own text names other must-precede relationships: the grounding-research reconciliation before any Phase-1+ slice; the Phase-6 register merge before the Phase-7 conformance census; the honest registry before the Phase-6 liveness sweep. But each of those is satisfied by the phase order as written, and none re-introduces atomic-merge coupling. Confidence: **HIGH** for the registry-before-checker edge. Note the scope of the claim: the survey above enumerates the other hard candidates rather than claiming uniqueness outright.

---

### Stress 2 — Is dropping the atomic coordinated move genuinely safe?

**The claim under test (D3).** The prior plan's machinery — "one coordinated atomic move, so reclassified bounds are never enforced nowhere" — is dropped. Band reclassification (Phase 1 Slice 1.3), the registry trim (Slice 1.4), and the spec amendments (Slice 1.5) land incrementally. The plan accepts a transient compile window in which the reclassified bands are enforced nowhere.

**What the atomicity machinery protected.** In a *running* product, reclassifying a band from prove-or-reject to a deferred-runtime-governance flag opens a window. During that window, the compiler has stopped rejecting the out-of-band write, but the runtime governance sweep that should now catch it does not yet exist. For that window, an out-of-band write is enforced by neither side. The coordinated move existed to collapse that window to zero by landing the compiler reclassification and the runtime enforcement together.

**Why the window is moot here — two independent reasons, either one sufficient.**

1. **The runtime enforces nothing regardless (the stub argument).** `Evaluator.Fire/Update` throw `NotImplementedException`; there is no governance sweep. Bands are enforced *nowhere at runtime* before the reclassification, and *nowhere at runtime* after it. The reclassification changes only the *compiler's* behavior: it stops false-rejecting merely-unprovable intervals. There is no "enforced-nowhere window" that the reclassification opens, because the runtime half of the window was already empty and stays empty until the separate runtime initiative lands. The coordinated move would couple a compiler change to a runtime change *that cannot land at all in this plan* — which would block the compiler half indefinitely. That indefinite block is exactly the alternative that D3's four-leg rationale rejected.
2. **No users (the blast-radius argument).** Even granting the window, the only entities that compile against this compiler are `samples/` and test fixtures, all in-tree and repaired in Phase 2 Slice 2.4. There is no deployed `.precept` definition governing real data whose out-of-band write could slip through. The window's blast radius is zero.

**Adversarial counter: does the window corrupt anything *inside* the plan?** The sharpest version of the worry: could a transient window in which a reclassified band is a warning rather than a rejection let a later phase build over a false-clean — a compile result that reads clean when it should not? Checked. The reclassification's compile-side exit criterion has three parts: an out-of-band write on a non-proof-carrying field compiles clean; a *provable* band violation emits the proven-violation warning; and the carries-proof set still rejects unprovable cases. The carries-proof set — the mincount `count>0` arm of Strategy 2 (collection-growth reasoning), plus sign-modifier and rule-fact preservation — **still rejects**. So the genuine-prevention floor is unbroken throughout the window. The only thing that changes is that *merely-unprovable* intervals stop being rejected, and that is the *intended* correction: those rejections were over-rejections, not prevention. Nothing downstream consumes "band rejected an unprovable interval" as a soundness premise. The Phase-2 checker reads the *registry*, not band verdicts, and Stress 1 already gates that edge on the registry being honest first. So the window corrupts nothing inside the plan.

**Verdict — SAFE.** Either the stub argument or the no-users argument alone suffices; both hold. The one residual transient — merely-unprovable intervals briefly warn instead of rejecting, before the runtime governance lane ever exists — is the *intended* behavior change, not a regression, and the carries-proof floor stays prove-or-reject throughout. Confidence: **HIGH**, grounded in the verified stub and the in-tree-only corpus.

---

### Stress 3 — Hunt for a missed cross-phase dependency

The incremental sequencing's risk is a dependency that *looks* soft but silently corrupts if violated. Three candidates earn a full probe.

**3a — The DesugarsToRule synthesis ↔ 2c-ii consumer coupling (Phase 1 Slice 1.2 ↔ Phase 5 2c-ii).** Phase 1 Slice 1.2 synthesizes each modifier's or bound's desugared form into `TypedRule`/`TypedEnsure` shapes in the typed model. Today that artifact has **zero constraint-synthesis or enforcement consumers, and 2 cosmetic/display consumers** — the grammar generator at `Program.cs:173` and the MCP formatter at `CatalogFormatters.cs:253+`. That count is Frank finding B2, a factual-accuracy fix from the adversarial review. The hazard: if 2c-ii's compile-time prove-or-reject path discharges purely via relational narrowing and *never reads the synthesized TypedRule*, then Slice 1.2 ships **dead model state** that nothing exercises until the deferred runtime sweep — a violation of the unconsumed-artifact guard, the rule that synthesized rules may land only if a real compile-time consumer reads them. **Is this a hard ordering?** No. It is a *decision* coupling, and both plans — the superseded prior plan and this one — flag it explicitly. At Slice 1.2's start, the executor must decide between two options: land the synthesis ahead of the sweep, or co-defer the synthesis together with the enforcement. The same caveat is re-asserted at the 2c-ii slice: "confirm 2c-ii's prove-or-reject actually consumes the synthesized TypedRule, not just relational narrowing." The guard converts a latent dead-state hazard into an explicit gate. **Verdict: SURFACED, not missed.** The coupling is named at both ends with a recorded decision, and the safe default under uncertainty — co-defer rather than ship an unconsumed half — keeps the plan sound. Residual risk: the decision is owed to the owner at slice start and could be wrongly resolved toward "land ahead" — but that is an execution-discipline risk, not a sequencing gap. Confidence: **MEDIUM**. The consumer question is genuinely open until 2c-ii's discharge path is built; the *guard* is HIGH-confidence, but the *resolution* is not yet known.

**3b — The pow-exponent misroute (Phase 1 Slice 1.4) ↔ pow-exponent message rendering (Phase 4).** Two phases touch pow-exponent fault handling. Slice 1.4 fixes the shape-keyed misroute — the rule that any `≥0` requirement routes to `SqrtOfNegative` — by moving to requirement-identity routing. Phase 4 fixes the per-shape message rendering. The hazard is *double-implementation*: both phases "fixing pow" would either conflict or leave one half stale. **Verdict: COORDINATED, not missed.** The plan pins the instruction "land once — coordinate with Phase 1 Slice 1.4" on the Phase-4 row. The registry routing lands in 1.4; the message rendering cross-references it. There is no build-order hazard, only a duplication hazard, and the merge-once instruction already fences it. Confidence: **HIGH**.

**3c — The choice-default-membership single-build (Phase 3 ↔ Phase 4).** Choice-default membership — the check that a choice field's default value is a member of the declared choice set — is routed to the **type checker**, not to a proof obligation, in Phase 3 Slice 3.3. The reason: routing it as an obligation would break the C59/C86 invariant — the compile-time guarantee, numbered C59/C86, that a Create from defaults always succeeds. The Phase-4 choice work — the diagnostic PRE0088 (`ChoiceElementTypeMismatch`, for a choice whose value literals do not match its declared element type), the diagnostic PRE0090 (`ChoiceMissingElementType`, for a choice declared without `of T`), plus the element-type trait gate (the check that gates a choice's declared element type on the `ChoiceElement` type trait, rather than rejecting an invalid element type only incidentally when its value literals fail to parse) — **cross-references** the Phase-3 route rather than rebuilding it. The hazard: if Phase 4 ran its choice work *before* Phase 3 wired the type-checker route, the Phase-4 author might re-derive default-membership as a proof obligation — the wrong home — and reintroduce the C59/C86 break. **Verdict: a SOFT ordering with a real (but bounded) trap.** It is not a *hard* correctness coupling, because running Phase 4 first does not silently corrupt a structural proof. It is a *single-build* coupling, where the wrong order invites a duplicate implementation in the wrong home. The plan mitigates in two ways: (i) it orders Phase 3 before Phase 4 in the phase summary, and (ii) it explicitly tags the Phase-4 choice row "cross-references Phase 3 Slice 3.3, not rebuilt." That is the correct mitigation for a single-build coupling — name the canonical home and the cross-reference. Confidence: **HIGH** that the mitigation is sufficient *if the cross-reference tags are honored*; **MEDIUM** that an executor under phase-parallelism pressure will not re-derive. That risk is worth a one-line execution reminder, not a re-sequence.

**Two more edges checked and cleared quickly:**

- **Corpus compat-wave timing (Phase 2 Slice 2.4 absorbs waves from Phases 1/2/4).** Three separate compat waves — batches of existing sample and fixture files that start correctly failing once a fix lands — break corpus fixtures: the position-totality wave from Phase 2, the band-reclassification false-accept fixes from Phase 1, and the soundness false-accept fixes from Phase 4. The plan buckets corpus repair as an explicit Phase-2 slice, delegated to the precept-author sub-agent, and notes that later waves are fixed "as they surface." Because there are no users, a fixture broken by a Phase-4 wave and not yet repaired is a harmless transient under D3, not a corruption. **Cleared** — the repair is demand-driven, not a hard predecessor of anything.
- **GATE-M (PRE0141 ownership) resolving as a Phase-5 side effect, consumed by Phase-6 Slice 6.4.** GATE-M is the owner-decision gate that decides which pipeline stage owns emission of PRE0141, the `UnprovedAssignmentQualifierCompatibility` diagnostic, whose bound-expression ownership is today split across stages. GATE-M is **not** a Phase-0 gate: it resolves at Phase-5 start, as a side effect of 2c-ii's bounds-desugar-to-rules work. Phase 6's ownership analyzer (Slice 6.4) — the build-time check that every diagnostic is emitted by exactly one owning stage — needs GATE-M resolved, because the analyzer carries exactly one carve-out (one exemption from the single-owner rule): the PRE0141 split ownership. If 2c-ii's bounds-desugar-to-rules work resolves PRE0141's ownership, that carve-out shrinks away and the analyzer ships with none. The plan orders Phase 5 before Phase 6 and instructs "confirm at Phase 5 start." **Cleared** — Phase 6 strictly follows Phase 5 in the summary, so the phase order satisfies the dependency. And even if GATE-M does not shrink the carve-out, the carve-out remains sound: its only defect is exempting more than strictly necessary, not exempting something wrongly.

**Two previously-unsurveyed hard orderings, now covered (both satisfied by phase order):**

- **Slice 0.4 → every Phase-1+ slice.** The grounding-research reconciliation is a hard internal ordering by the plan's own text: reconcile the stale grounding doc before any Phase-1+ slice feeds an agent. It was absent from this hunt's earlier survey. It is satisfied by the phase order as written — Phase 0 precedes all Phase-1+ work — so no re-sequencing follows. But the ordering is hard, not soft.
- **Slice 6.7 → Slice 7.1.** The Phase-6 register merge must exist before the Phase-7 conformance census reads it. Slice 7.1's exit criterion — read the one merged register, do not stand up a second tracker — is unsatisfiable before 6.7 lands. The Phase-7 header names this hard; the phase order satisfies it.

**Verdict — no missed cross-phase dependency.** Every inter-phase edge that could silently corrupt is one of three things: (a) the one hard ordering, established in Stress 1; (b) a surfaced decision or coordination coupling whose guard is already in the plan (3a, 3b, 3c); or (c) a demand-driven or phase-order-satisfied soft edge. The incremental sequencing does not hide an all-or-nothing coupling. Confidence: **MEDIUM-HIGH**. The search covered every named inter-phase edge, but a red-team cannot prove the *absence* of an unnamed one. What bounds the residual is the structural argument: only Phase 2 bakes a property over the registry's shape, and only single-build couplings risk wrong-home duplication — and all of those are tagged.

---

### Stress 4 — Does any RETAINED slice assert a runtime-EXECUTION exit criterion, and is every admitted fault-safe-but-runtime-dependent operation's enforcement captured in § 11? (no-silent-loss hunt)

This is the most important probe for the boundary. Under the split established in §0 discipline 3 — prove-or-reject for genuine safety questions, admit-now / defer-enforcement for operations that are safe provided the runtime enforces something — **admitting a fault-safe operation whose enforcement defers is the correct pre-release behavior**, not a leak. The runtime is a pure stub and nothing executes, so an admitted-but-unenforced obligation creates no live fault (D3), as long as its enforcement is captured in §11, the Runtime-Coding Handoff enumeration. The genuine risks are two: (a) a retained exit criterion that asserts a runtime-**execution** outcome, which would be a disguised boundary violation; or (b) an admitted fault-safe operation whose deferred enforcement is **not** captured with a capture pointer, which would be a silent loss. Walking the slices that touch a runtime-adjacent surface:

- **Band reclassification (Phase 1 Slice 1.3).** The exit criterion has three parts: an out-of-band write on a non-proof-carrying field *compiles clean*; a *provable* band violation *emits the proven-violation warning*; and the carries-proof set still *rejects*. All three are `precept_compile` / `dotnet build` outcomes. The slice never asserts "governance refuses the write" — that is the deferred enforcement half, captured in `evaluator.md §7.6`. **No leak.** This is the admit-now model working as intended: the compiler admits the fault-safe operation, and admitting a merely-unprovable interval at compile time does not create a guaranteed fault — it defers a governance decision the runtime owns.

- **D8 dimension-narrowed / commensurable cross-unit assignment (Phase 4).** D8 is the locked canonical-doc decision (in `docs/language/business-domain-types.md`) governing assignment of a quantity in one unit to a field declared in a commensurable unit of the same dimension. The *static-fold subset* — both operands compile-time constant — is compiler-pure. The *dynamic-write case* is **also fault-safe**: target-directed conversion of a commensurable same-dimension quantity multiplies by a finite UCUM scale factor. That is a total operation — it succeeds for every input, so it implicates no fault family, and there is nothing in it a fully-built runtime could fail to perform. The only overflow family involved is the PARKED representability family from owner decision D1, which is never a rejection ground anywhere in the plan — this is not a D8-specific waiver. A fully-built runtime performs the conversion, so the compiler **admits** the dynamic-write case now, and the conversion's **enforcement defers** to `evaluator.md §Intake-Boundary` — the same admit-now / defer-enforcement treatment bands get. **No leak.** The admission creates no live fault pre-release, because the runtime is stubbed, and the deferred enforcement carries a §11 capture pointer — verify the pointer exists, so there is no silent loss. A deepening pass — the later planning pass that expands a later phase's summary rows into fully specified slices when that phase becomes current — must keep the enforcement captured. It must also not re-introduce the over-rejection this treatment removed: the compiler must not go back to rejecting the fault-safe dynamic-write case merely because the runtime that will perform the conversion is not yet built.

- **2c-ii field-reference bound enforcement (Phase 5).** The exit criterion — flagged by Frank — is: assert **prove-or-reject at compile time** via `Compiler.Compile`, and *never* "a running entity's out-of-bound write is refused." The runtime ingress enforcement is the deferred tail, captured at `evaluator.md §7.6`. **No leak.** The exit is a compile-time discharge verdict, and the unconsumed-artifact guard (Stress 3a) additionally ensures the synthesized rule is either consumed at compile time or co-deferred.

- **Write-aware tier-2 discharge (Phase 3 Slice 3.1).** Tier-2 discharge here is the proof-engine sense: discharging an obligation from a standing declared fact rather than from a guard next to the expression. The slice's genuine compiler work is **intra-chain (same-transaction)** write-awareness. A stale intra-chain discharge is real fault-unprovability — the compiler can SEE the prefix writes — so the compiler **rejects**. That is permanent prove-or-reject; no exit criterion asserts a runtime outcome. The committed-state discharge — proving against values persisted by earlier, separate operations — is *sound once the runtime enforces the discharging constraints at every prior commit*. Its soundness is gated on a runtime capability, not on a property the compiler can never prove. So the compiler **admits** it now, relying on that captured enforcement obligation, and the enforcement **defers** — admit-now / defer-enforcement, the same treatment bands get. **No leak.** No exit criterion asserts a runtime-execution outcome; the deferred enforcement carries a §11 capture pointer (`evaluator.md §7.6 + §10`); and pre-release the admission creates no live fault (D3).

- **BUG-030 UCUM cancellation (Phase 3 / Phase 4 compile half).** BUG-030 is the filed bug that unit cancellation ignores UCUM scale factors. The compile half applies the kg→g ×1000 factor in the proof engine's interval and cancellation paths, so the engine stops admitting magnitudes outside proven intervals — a compile-time interval-arithmetic fix. The *value application* — `4.00 USD/ft × 36 in → 12.00` — is the deferred runtime half, captured in `evaluator.md`'s cross-unit reduction-rule section. **No leak.** The retained half tightens the *proven interval*, which is a compile-time soundness fix; it does not compute a runtime value.

- **Derived-value type derivations (Phase 4: money/money, quantity/quantity, inverse division, money×exchangerate).** Type derivation and overload selection are compiler-pure and retained. The *value computation* defers to `evaluator.md`'s reduction-rule section, with a per-family capture line. **No leak.** The retained half produces a *type*, verifiable via `precept_compile`; the value tail is captured, not built. The prior plan had a silent-loss gap exactly here: findings BIZ-10/11/12/13 — BIZ-numbered findings are rows of the business-domain audit, the finding register the Phase-4 business-domain work answers — named "runtime value defers" with no capture obligation attached. The per-family capture lines close that gap, so the defer is honest rather than a dropped tail.

- **Phase 2 Slice 2.5 / Phase 7 conformance runtime facet.** Both are explicitly CANON_DESIGN work or authored-as-Skip'd test cases. Slice 2.5 *records* a runtime precondition; the Phase-7 runtime facet *authors* an acceptance spec as skipped test cases. Neither asserts a runtime-execution outcome. The conformance runtime facet's cases ARE the spec; making them green is deferred runtime coding. **No leak.**

- **`RestoreConstraintsFailed` DU removal (Phase 7 / Phase 0 Slice 0.3).** Verified: this is a live API-surface discriminated-union subtype at `RestoreOutcome.cs:18`, consumed by `Precept.cs:121`. Its removal is a *pure API-surface type edit* — classified COMPILER_CODE, landable now, and **not** runtime coding. It does not require Restore to execute; it shrinks the type the stubbed Restore would eventually return. **No leak.** The item is correctly classified as compiler work despite living on a runtime-adjacent type.

**Verdict — no runtime-execution exit criterion, no silent loss, in any retained slice.** Every runtime-adjacent slice that stays in scope satisfies one of two conditions: (a) it has a strictly compile-time exit criterion, verifiable via `precept_compile`, `dotnet build`, or `Compiler.Compile`; or (b) it admits a fault-safe operation — the D8 dynamic-write, the committed-state discharge — with its **enforcement** deferred to a named `docs/runtime/*.md` capture pointer. That is admit-now / defer-enforcement, creating no live fault pre-release (D3). The deepening pass must preserve this property: **a deepened exit criterion must not "complete" a slice by asserting the runtime-execution outcome its deferred half owns, and every admitted fault-safe operation must keep its §11 enforcement capture.** The operational test is simple: every retained exit criterion stays verifiable with `Evaluator.Fire/Update/InspectFire/InspectUpdate/Restore` still throwing `NotImplementedException`. Confidence: **HIGH** — each slice's exit criterion was checked against the verified stub.

---

### Red-team verdict

The 8-phase ordering survives all four stress tests:

1. **The one scheduling-binding hard dependency holds.** The registry must be honest — which requires both the band reclassification (Slice 1.3) and the registry repartition (Slice 1.4) — before the Phase-2 structural checker asserts against it, because the checker permanently locks in the live registry's shape the first time it runs. The plan's own text names other must-precede relationships — Slice 0.4 before any Phase-1+ slice; the honest registry before the Phase-6 liveness sweep; the Phase-6 register merge before the Phase-7 conformance census — but each is satisfied by the phase order as written. Every remaining inter-phase edge is a soft quality-ordering or a surfaced decision/coordination coupling, and none creates a new false-clean — a spuriously clean compile result that later work would build on as if it were sound. (HIGH)
2. **Dropping the atomic coordinated move is safe.** The runtime enforces nothing regardless — the stub is verified — *and* there are no users. The carries-proof floor stays prove-or-reject throughout the transient window, and the window only flips merely-unprovable intervals from over-rejection to warning, which is the intended correction. (HIGH)
3. **No missed cross-phase dependency.** The DesugarsToRule↔2c-ii consumer coupling, the pow-exponent land-once coordination, and the choice-default single-build are all surfaced, with guards or cross-reference tags already in the plan. The corpus compat-wave and GATE-M edges are demand-driven or satisfied by the phase order. (MEDIUM-HIGH)
4. **No runtime-execution exit criterion and no silent loss in any retained slice.** Every runtime-adjacent in-scope slice either has a compile-time exit criterion, or admits a fault-safe operation — the D8 dynamic-write, the committed-state discharge — with its enforcement deferred under a named §11 capture pointer. That is admit-now / defer-enforcement, with no live fault pre-release. (HIGH)

**Two execution-discipline follow-ups to carry forward.** These are guardrails for the executor, not re-sequencing:

- **The 3a resolution is owed to the owner at the start of Phase 1 Slice 1.2** — either land the DesugarsToRule synthesis ahead of the sweep, or co-defer it. The safe default under uncertainty is co-defer: do not ship an unconsumed half. Re-confirm at the Phase-5 2c-ii slice that its discharge actually reads the synthesized `TypedRule`.
- **The 3c single-build couplings must honor their cross-reference tags.** Choice-default membership (type checker, Phase 3) and pow-exponent routing (registry, Phase 1) each have exactly one canonical build home, and the cross-referencing phase (Phase 4) must not re-derive them. A one-line execution reminder suffices; no re-sequence is warranted.

The ordering is **sound under the boundary**: no retained slice depends on runtime execution, the one hard ordering is honored, and the incremental sequencing that the atomicity relaxation enables introduces no hidden all-or-nothing coupling.

---

## 12. Definition of Done

The whole plan converges on one gate, and this section states it: **the compiler is finished, every runtime design decision is fully captured in the runtime canon, and the runtime coding itself is handed off — not built.** The gate is deliberately redefined for the compiler/runtime boundary set in §0. Every criterion below is one of two kinds. Either it is compile-time-verifiable — checkable with `dotnet build`, `dotnet test`, or `precept_compile` while the runtime stays a stub — or it is a design-capture assertion, meaning the criterion is satisfied when a named `docs/runtime/*.md` section holds the required design. **No criterion asserts a runtime-execution outcome.** There is no "governance sweep enforces bounds" criterion, no "`Fire` returns X" criterion, no "the magnitude cancels to 12.00" criterion, and no criterion asserting the soundness of committed-state discharge. Every one of those runtime-execution outcomes lives instead in §11 (Runtime-Coding Handoff), where it is authored as the acceptance spec for the deferred runtime initiative — written down as what the future build must satisfy, never asserted green here.

The gate is met when ALL ten of the criteria below hold. Each criterion names the phase or phases that produce it and the artifact that verifies it, so a reviewer can audit completion after the fact.

### DoD-1 — The fault floor is SOUND at compile time

This criterion says the proof engine no longer records any proof it cannot actually back. Every False-Proved hole — a proof-ledger entry that marked an obligation discharged when the fault was not actually ruled out — is closed. Where the proof engine cannot prove a fault absent, it **rejects the definition**: the prevention posture is that an unprovable case is rejected, never skipped, and never falsely marked discharged. Specifically, the following must all be true.

- The write-aware handling of tier-2 discharge within a single transaction has shipped (Phase 3 Slice 3.1). Tier-2 discharge means proving an obligation from a standing constraint — a modifier fact or a declared rule fact; the intra-chain fix makes such a discharge invalid when a write earlier in the same event's action chain invalidates the standing fact.
- The qualifier-source mutation obligation is live (Phase 3 Slice 3.2). This is the compile-time obligation created on every mutation of a field that is referenced as a qualifier source: the compiler must prove that the values whose qualifiers depend on that field are re-established after the mutation, or it rejects.
- The remaining known holes are each dispositioned (Phase 3 Slice 3.3): the presence-wrapping contradiction (a presence obligation wrapped inside a contradictory guard must stay unproven and reject, instead of being falsely marked proved); the mincount-at-birth ledger seed (the seed fact that lets the proof ledger report a minimum-count bound as proved at Create even though the collection is empty at birth and cannot satisfy a minimum count); the totality of computed arguments in the FunctionArg position (the argument slot of a function or member call — for example, the pow exponent); choice-default membership (routed through the type checker); and three open bugs — BUG-021 (min-only containment: re-verifying which min-only band residue genuinely carries proof and survives the band scale-back), BUG-028 (default-satisfiability fold completeness: the fold under-emits, which is the safe direction — a missed emission never introduces a false proof), and BUG-029 (satisfiability diagnostics render the raw `TypedExpression` AST in user-facing messages — a message-quality fix).
- The compile-time half of BUG-030 — the UCUM (Unified Code for Units of Measure) unit-cancellation bug — applies the catalog conversion factor in the interval and cancellation proof paths (Phase 3 Slice 3.3).
- No obligation family routes through the generic `_ => DivisionByZero` fallback arm; every family has its own honest fault-code routing. The residual hand-written switch in `CreateFaultSiteLink` shrank rather than grew as part of that work (Phase 1 Slice 1.4, applying finding A3 from the adversarial plan review). The mislabeled "vacuously proved" entries are replaced by an explicit `ProofStrategy.Vacuous`.
- **Verified by:** the proof-engine `dotnet test` suites pass, and `precept_compile` run against the corpus cases that previously produced False-Proved entries now rejects each of them with an honest message.
- **Boundary carve-out — this is a deliberate scope line, not a gap.** The soundness of tier-2 discharge against committed-state values — values persisted by earlier, separate operations, so cross-event and cross-transaction — rests on a captured runtime obligation (`evaluator.md §7.6 + §10`): the invariant that the runtime enforced every constraint at every prior commit. It is NOT a compiler exit criterion. The compiler **admits** the committed-state discharge now, relying on that captured obligation, and defers its enforcement to the runtime — the admit-now / defer-enforcement treatment, the same treatment the band checks receive. The compiler does not hold an over-rejection open while waiting for the runtime. The genuine compile-time prove-or-reject piece — that a stale same-transaction discharge **rejects** — ships in Phase 3 Slice 3.1.

### DoD-2 — The fault floor is POSITION-TOTAL and COMPLETE for the non-parked families

This criterion says the obligation-creation walk misses no position: no expression slot exists where a fault can hide because no obligation was ever created there.

- The catalog's `ChildExpressionPositions` projection (built in Phase 2 Slice 2.1) is the single source of truth for child-expression positions across the 5 expression-bearing discriminated unions. The now-total obligation-creation walk closes all **10 fail-open positions** — the child positions (guards, `because`-message holes, member-call arguments) the walk previously never visited (Phase 2 Slice 2.2). That slice bundles intra-guard short-circuit narrowing in the same change, so that closing the positions does not mass-false-reject the common optional-field idiom. Bugs BUG-031 and BUG-032 are closed by this work.
- The 3-layer creation-exhaustiveness checker gates the compiler's own build (Phase 2 Slice 2.3). After it lands, a fail-open obligation position at any catalogued child-expression slot is **structurally impossible**: the build fails whenever `{declared child-expression positions} − {positions the walk recurses into} ≠ ∅`. The 6 latent no-default-arm holes — the additional fail-open child-expression positions that compile today only because nothing has tripped them, all rooted in the obligation-creation walk's switch having no default arm — are closed so the checker passes green.
- **Verified by:** `dotnet build` fails when a fail-open position is deliberately injected — the checker is a build-time gate, and a red fixture demonstrates it — and `precept_compile` confirms the closed positions now create obligations.
- **Honesty carve-out (D1, owner-disclosed):** decimal and temporal representability overflow — a computed value exceeding what the number or date types can represent — is a documented known limitation and is **never claimed prevented**. The checker closes every other fault lane. Representability is not a position the checker must cover, because it is PARKED by owner decision D1, not fail-open.

### DoD-3 — The fault floor is RIGHT-SIZED (compiler half) and canon is consistent with shipped behavior

This criterion covers the band split (owner decision D2), the registry-honesty fix, and the spec amendments that keep the canonical docs truthful about both.

- The band-containment lane — PRE0078 for declared-band `IntervalContainment`, PRE0135 for `LengthContainment`, PRE0136 for `CountContainment` — is reclassified from fault-floor prove-or-reject to a proven-violation warning for non-proof-carrying fields (Phase 1 Slice 1.3, applying D2). A proven-violation warning fires only when the compiler can actually prove the band is violated; merely-unprovable cases compile clean. The carries-proof set — bands whose declared bound feeds a compile-time safety proof, namely the mincount `count>0` Strategy-2 arm and sign-modifier / rule-fact preservation — **survives** with prove-or-reject intact. PRE0078's "representable range" message no longer attaches to a band check.
- The fault-code registry is trimmed and repartitioned whole, in one pass (Phase 1 Slice 1.4). This is the plan's one HARD ordering: the registry must be honest **before** the Phase-2 creation-exhaustiveness checker first reads it, because the checker permanently encodes whatever routing the registry holds at that moment. The pass removes `LengthBoundViolation` and `CountBoundViolation` from `[StaticallyPreventable]` and from the bijective core — Length and Count only, because OutOfRange was never in the bijective core (adversarial-review finding A2). It adds `IndexOutOfBounds`, `KeyNotFound`, and the UnexpectedNull bijective row. It does **not** add a `FaultCode.TemporalOverflow`. It **keeps** `NumericOverflow` as a documented known-limitation backstop, without retargeting it.
- The owner-approved spec amendment set — the GATE-F decision gate, whose enumeration was completed by adversarial-review finding B1 — is applied complete (Phase 1 Slice 1.5). The set: spec §0.6 lines L256/L258 plus §0.7 line L266; §0.6 item-6 (L208); the Principle 11 "constraint range impossibility" claim; and the L112 "defensive redundancy" clause, quoted verbatim in the amendment, together with the ruling that governance is not a fault check. After the amendment, canon no longer asserts band prove-or-reject for the reclassified families. The three-category taxonomy — every diagnostic is exactly one of fault floor, definition incoherence, or flag layer (decision gate GATE-H) — is recorded in canon. `fault-system.md` matches `FaultCode.cs`: the 13-versus-15 count discrepancy (finding RC-09 in the registry-corrections input register) and the stale `NullInNonNullableContext` partner entry are both reconciled.
- **Verified by:** `precept_compile` now admits a previously-false-rejected out-of-band write on a non-proof-carrying field — it compiles clean or with the warning only; `dotnet build` is green; and a doc grep confirms that no sentence asserting band prove-or-reject survives anywhere in the amendment set that finding B1 enumerated.
- **Runtime half deferred — captured, not done here:** the runtime governance enforcement of the reclassified bounds is captured in `evaluator.md §7.6` as a recoverable governance refusal — the write refused and the in-flight working copy discarded as a normal structured outcome, **NOT** `EventOutcome.Faulted` — and handed off through §11. **No DoD criterion asserts that this enforcement runs.**

### DoD-4 — Overflow + maxplaces honestly disclosed as known limitations (D1 PARKED)

This criterion is about honesty, not construction. Owner decision D1 parks the overflow families; this criterion requires that the parking is disclosed everywhere and claimed prevented nowhere.

- Four things are PARKED — deliberately not built, and **never claimed prevented**: decimal-representability overflow; temporal-arithmetic overflow (the same representability genus); the maxplaces compile-time precision proof; and the number-model redesign. The representability obligation family is not constructed at any sink. There is no bounds-checking for it and no multi-field magnitude proof.
- Canon states the limitation honestly. Spec §0.7 and Principle 11 are softened to a disclosed known limitation (Phase 1 Slice 1.5). `fault-system.md` documents `NumericOverflow` as accepted-not-prevented. The maxplaces honesty note records that over-precision is jointly **unenforced**, on two grounds together: the compile-time precision proof is parked, AND the runtime's two maxplaces enforcement points — numbered points 2 and 3 at `business-domain-types.md:1581-1590`, namely precision enforcement on event arguments at ingress and precision enforcement on an arithmetic result as it is set — are deferred. Neither the compiler nor the stub runtime catches over-precision (recorded as the Phase 4 PARKED note).
- **Verified by:** a grep confirming that no canon sentence claims overflow, representability, or maxplaces precision *prevented*. Per adversarial-review finding B1, the grep is **scoped over `docs/` AND `src/Precept.Analyzers/*.cs` analyzer message/description strings AND the `Diagnostics.cs` / `Faults.cs` *prevention*-claim strings** — not `docs/` alone, because the catalog enforces the invariant in code, which makes the in-code strings part of the completeness check. Within that scope, the `PRECEPT0002` analyzer — the build-time completeness analyzer that requires every `FaultCode` member to carry the `[StaticallyPreventable]` attribute naming its preventing diagnostic — no longer claims universal delivered prevention. Its message text satisfies one of two shapes. Either it asserts only the linkage invariant — every fault code must reference its preventing diagnostic, with no claim that the fault is thereby unreachable at runtime — or it carries an explicit `known-not-prevented` carve-out for `NumericOverflow`, the one parked member for which the fault-to-diagnostic linkage still stands but the delivered guarantee does not. The known-limitation note exists, with the temporal-overflow probe evidence from Phase 0 Slice 0.1 recorded in it.
- **Owner-gated surface — see DoD-9.** This is the plan's one genuine **relaxation**: a disclosed parked gap in prevention. Keep it distinct from the band change, which per adversarial-review finding A6 is a **relocation** — prevention moves from compile-time rejection to future runtime governance, and is not weakened. `philosophy.md` is not auto-edited for either.

### DoD-5 — The canonical spec is 100% IMPLEMENTED across the compiler surface

This criterion says the compiler implements everything the locked language spec describes — the readiness plan's core mission.

- All **236 spec-coverage gaps** are closed. The arithmetic: Lexer (6) + Primitives/choice (22) + Business (28) + Temporal (11) = 67, delivered in Phase 4; Collections (22) + Modifiers (11) + Expression (23) + Rules/ensures (18) + States/actions (25) + Binder/TC (17) + Graph (10) = 126, delivered in Phase 5; Proof/diagnostic (28) + pipeline-tail gap-fill (15) = 43, delivered in Phase 6. 67 + 126 + 43 = 236.
- The flagship soundness-grade items among those gaps are fixed: the silent-zero exponent-value bug; the cross-counting-unit PRE0137 false-accept; the choice field-versus-field false-clean; and the hard error on the flagship temporal form date ± quantity (Phases 4–5).
- The field-reference bound enforcement half of the locked relational-rules-and-bounds design — the 2c-ii half, tracked as BUG-020 and the largest unbuilt locked design — ships as compile-time prove-or-reject (Phase 5 Slice 5.1). Its exit is asserted via `Compiler.Compile`, **never** via a running entity's refusal. The unconsumed-artifact guard is satisfied: either a named compile-time consumer reads the synthesized `TypedRule`, or the synthesis is deferred together with the runtime governance sweep.
- **Verified by:** `precept_compile` over the sample corpus plus the spec-coverage-audit fixtures; the corpus compat wave — the existing sample and fixture files that start correctly failing once soundness fixes land — repaired (Phase 2 Slice 2.4, plus the Phase 7 Slice 7.5 sample-corpus work, delegated to the `precept-author` agent); and `dotnet test` green.

### DoD-6 — Diagnostic + emission-ownership completeness

This criterion says every declared diagnostic is real — it fires or it is retired — and every emission site is owned by the right pipeline stage, enforced by build-time analyzers.

- Every declared `DiagnosticCode` satisfies emit-or-retire: it demonstrably fires, or it is explicitly retired with its enum member removed. The count reconciliation squares the tally of declared diagnostic codes against the tally with verified emission sites. The prior plan carried that figure as "148 versus 162"; Phase 6 Slice 6.1 flags it as stale and recomputes it against HEAD — the canonical registry at `diagnostic-system.md:404` currently states 164 active codes. The reconciliation runs through `DiagnosticCoverageScanner` Patterns 1/2/2b **plus hand-enumerated dynamically-dispatched emission sites**. The scanner's patterns are: Pattern 1, which finds literal `Diagnostics.Create` call sites; and Patterns 2 and 2b, which find catalog-mediated codes — those carried on the `CIDiagnosticCode`, `FormatErrorCode`, `SemanticErrorCode`, and `ProofRequirementMeta` fields. The scanner has no dynamic-dispatch pattern, so a literal grep for `Diagnostics.Create` is incomplete (a known project pitfall). The stale list of roughly 18 no-emission codes is re-derived against HEAD rather than trusted (Phase 6 Slice 6.1).
- Every `[StaticallyPreventable]` code is verified live-not-dead: the WHOLE family is enumerated and each member gets an explicit disposition, because a dead preventable code is a hole in Principle 11 — a guarantee claimed with nothing backing it (Phase 6 Slice 6.2).
- The single-stage diagnostic-ownership Roslyn analyzer gates emission: every `Diagnostics.Create` site's stage must equal `GetMeta(code).Stage`, with exactly one carve-out and a zero-entry allow-list. The carve-out is PRE0141 (`UnprovedAssignmentQualifierCompatibility`) as emitted at the `TypedConditional` position — the one diagnostic whose bound-expression stage ownership is split, which is the question decision gate GATE-M resolves. Per adversarial-review finding A3, the analyzer works from the attribute-derived bijective map, not from growing the `CreateFaultSiteLink` hand-switch (Phase 6 Slice 6.4). The BUG-022 analyzer, which enforces exhaustive consumer switches over the `*Kind` enums, also ships (Phase 6 Slice 6.5).
- **Verified by:** both analyzers compile as build-time gates — `dotnet build` fails on an injected wrong-stage emission and on an injected non-exhaustive `*Kind` switch — and the emit-or-retire census exists as a meta-test.

### DoD-7 — Every runtime decision the compiler forced is CAPTURED in runtime canon

This criterion is entirely design-capture: the deferred runtime build must inherit a complete spec, with nothing the compiler work decided left only in someone's head. All of the following are captured across Phases 0, 3, 4, and 5 and confirmed in Phase 7.

- The fault-delivery contract is reconciled to ONE shape. This is decision gate GATE-I: the currently-divergent statements at `result-types.md:51` ↔ `:104/:117/:127` ↔ `evaluator.md §7.6/§9` ↔ `fault-system.md` Q1 must agree.
- OD-1 — the constraint that the runtime evaluator and compile-time constant folding share ONE expression-evaluation core (`ConstantFold`) — is recorded.
- The governance/ingress sweep is authored as ONE coherent constraint-plan-and-sweep design at `evaluator.md §7.6`, following the consolidation discipline — a single design, not 8-plus uncoordinated edits. Its contents: the DesugarsToRule constraint plan and its enforcement; the recoverable refusal for reclassified bands; the committed-state invariant; the collect-all sweep; cross-transition ensure; element-ingress governance; the 2c-ii ingress enforcement; omit/readonly enforcement; and the umbrella ingress sweep over all of them.
- The cross-unit reduction rule is written into `evaluator.md` — the capture the rest of the plan (the §11 handoff rows, the Coverage Ledger, and the Phase 3/4 doc-touch lists) cross-references by the identifier MO-DSO-05, and the highest-priority extraction that was never written down: the section specifying how the runtime computes a value when a price's unit differs from the quantity's unit (for example, 4.00 USD per foot times 36 inches yields 12.00). The rule: `k = ScaleToBase(u_q)/ScaleToBase(u_p)` computed in decimal, target-directed, rounding at maxplaces, with `k=1` in the same-unit case. Alongside it, the BRANCH_FALSE/BRANCH_TRUE/if-lowering opcode contract is captured — the contract for lowering and executing conditional expressions (the if-form and the short-circuit `&&`/`||`/`?:` operators). That opcode contract is the runtime half of the conditional-totality spec, the two-halved specification governing how conditional expressions are handled; the spec's compile-time half — the proof engine's conditional-totality narrowing — ships in Phase 5 Slice 5.2.
- Also captured: the D8 target-directed conversion contract at `evaluator.md §Intake-Boundary` — the deferred enforcement for the dynamic-write case the compiler now admits, under the admit-now / defer-enforcement treatment; a single canonical home for Point C, the question of which outcome type owns each external-input value violation (InvalidArgs versus ConstraintsFailed versus Faulted); the D9 injectable per-operation clock seam; the edge-value execution contract — the executed-value semantics covering banker's rounding, the left/right/mid clamp directions, and the ∞/NaN and integer-conversion-overflow fault surfaces — together with the temporal execution contract; and the Restore dissolution note — the runtime-canon note at `evaluator.md §7.1` recording that the Restore operation substantially dissolves as a distinct validation concern: restored data is trusted as valid at the time it was persisted and is not re-validated at restore time, the next operation through the contract re-governs it, and a structural-consistency trap is kept as the backstop for out-of-contract values.
- **Verified by:** Phase 7 Slice 7.6 confirms that each named `docs/runtime/*.md` section is present, internally consistent, and consolidated — in particular that §7.6 is ONE design — and that the standing declines (convex polyhedra and predicate abstraction kept off the proof path) carry full four-leg rationale in `proof-engine.md`.

### DoD-8 — The runtime CODING backlog is ENUMERATED as an explicit handoff

This criterion says the deferred work is a real backlog, not a pile of loose ends.

- §11 enumerates every item classified RUNTIME_CODE_DEFER, and each row carries its capture pointer into the runtime canon. A deferral with no capture pointer is a silent loss and is disallowed; there is no orphaned deferral.
- The runtime-behavior portion of the conformance suite is authored as the Skip'd v1 acceptance spec (Phase 7 Slice 7.2). The skipped cases ARE the spec; making them green is runtime coding and is therefore deferred.
- The admit-now / defer-enforcement split holds across the whole handoff. Every operation the compiler admits as fault-safe only because the runtime will enforce something — the D8 dynamic-write case, the committed-state discharge, and the bands — has a Skip'd runtime-ENFORCEMENT test case authored: the case asserts that the deferred governance or conversion executes and refuses on a real violation. The Skip'd case asserts the deferred *enforcement*; it is never a runtime admission that would flip a rejection the compiler is holding. Genuine prove-or-reject cases reject permanently and need no runtime partner case.
- **Verified by:** Phase 7 Slice 7.7 — each §11 row has a verified pointer, the `evaluator.md §7.6` captures are confirmed consolidated, and every confirmed-out or parked item carries its reason.

### DoD-9 — Owner-gated philosophy surfaces SURFACED (not auto-edited) — TERMINAL on owner deliberation

Two genuine philosophy-gap surfaces open now that overflow is a disclosed limitation under D1. Both are owner-gated: they are **surfaced, never auto-edited** in any implementation pass. This criterion is met when each gap has been surfaced to the owner with the gap framed, and reconciled **only** under explicit owner approval — riding the separate guarantee-statement promotion, never bundled into an implementation PR, per the CLAUDE.md philosophy rule.

- **The `philosophy.md` absolute-claims reconciliation.** The locked claims that overflow is a structural, compile-time impossibility now conflict with the disclosed limitation. The sites to surface, verified against HEAD: **L53** ("Division by zero, arithmetic overflow, empty collection access … are compile-time impossibilities"), **L61** ("division by zero, overflow, empty collection access … before any entity exists"), **L49/L51** (the "absolute" prevention framing), and **L57** ("a definition that compiles without diagnostics has no unproven evaluation faults"). The framing for the owner conversation is the one adversarial-review finding A6 supplied: **bands are relocated** — prevention moves from compile time to runtime governance and is not weakened — while **overflow is genuinely relaxed** — a disclosed parked gap. Do not blur the two. **Do not edit `philosophy.md` without explicit owner approval.**
- **The `evaluator.md §10` "no diagnostics ⇒ no faults" narrowing.** The headline non-negotiable — stated at `evaluator.md:19` and in the §10 correspondence table at `evaluator.md:1679`: "If the compiler emits no errors, the evaluator should never fault" — is now **false for the parked representability family**: the evaluator CAN fault on representability overflow even after a clean compile. The contract must be amended to except decimal/temporal representability overflow as a documented known limitation. The position-totality work of DoD-2 closes every *other* fault lane, so the contract holds for everything except the parked family. This is a **guarantee-surface narrowing** — surface it to the owner; do not unilaterally weaken the headline guarantee.
- **Verified by:** the consultation evidence is visible in the record — what was checked, what was found, what the owner authorized — and grep-able after the fact, per the Pre-Design Owner Consultation gate; `philosophy.md` is untouched in every implementation PR; and the `evaluator.md §10` carve-out lands only under recorded owner direction.

### DoD-10 — Owner sign-off on the Phase-0 gate set is TERMINAL and required

The plan promotes from status `Draft plan — 2026-06-16` to `Locked` **only when the Phase-0 gate set clears** (applying adversarial-review finding A1, the front-matter status-field correction). Owner sign-off is the terminal criterion: the gate is not "done" until the owner signs off, and the Phase-0 decision gates are the floor that everything downstream assumes.

- Every Phase-0 gate has a recorded disposition — either owner direction, or an explicit "defer to Phase N start" with a reason — before any code-bearing slice proceeds. The canonical gate roster: GATE-B/C/D/E/F/G/H/J/K/L/N/O (raised in Slice 0.2) plus GATE-I (raised in Slice 0.3). The same recorded-disposition requirement covers the probe dispositions — the recorded outcomes of the three read-only Phase 0 Slice 0.1 probes: the temporal-arithmetic overflow probe, the omit-with-global-rule join adjudication, and the PRE0101 false-emptiness confirmation — and the runtime-canon reconciliations: OD-1 (the shared expression-evaluation core), Point C (outcome-type ownership), D9 (the clock seam), the standing declines, and Restore. GATE-M — the question of which single pipeline stage owns the bound-expression emission of diagnostic PRE0141 — is deliberately not a Phase-0 gate: it resolves at Phase-5 start as a side effect of the 2c-ii work, with a carry-to-Phase-6 fallback.
- GATE-F is a Tier-3 consultation — it amends a locked spec, the heaviest consultation depth. The enumeration of amendment sites from adversarial-review finding B1 is complete **before** the owner conversation, and the amendment lands only on owner approval. GATE-A — the decision gate on whether to build overflow prevention — is recorded as already resolved: park, per owner decision D1.
- The grounding-research reconciliation lands **before** any Phase-1-or-later slice is fed to an agent. This is the one hard ordering internal to Phase 0, and it exists because a stale source doc contaminates every agent that reads it. The edit set for `fault-floor-definition-2026-06-11.md`: the family count changes from 11 to 9, because both representability families — family 9 (decimal) and family 10 (temporal) — move out per D1; the prove-or-reject carve-out; the GATE-A reframe; the wording fix from adversarial-review finding B2; the §10 honesty narrowing; and the rewrite of the tradeoff leg of the four-leg rationale.
- **Final owner sign-off** on the redefined runtime-ready gate (Phase 7 Slice 7.7) is the last act. Without it the gate is **not** met.

### Coverage guarantee — every input register row lands in a DoD criterion

The Definition of Done is the consolidating lens over the plan's input registers — the eight enumerated finding lists (Coverage Ledger §A–§H) the plan must account for. Nothing is dropped silently: every row has a disposition, and the full row-by-row placement lives in the § Coverage Ledger. The DoD-level reconciliation:

| Input register | Count | Where it lands in the DoD | Disposition |
|---|---|---|---|

**Explicitly NOT in the gate** — these are the deferred runtime-execution acceptance items, all living in §11: "governance sweep enforces bounds"; "`Fire`/`Update`/`InspectFire`/`InspectUpdate`/`Restore` returns X"; the soundness of committed-state cross-event discharge; "the magnitude cancels to 12.00"; element-ingress governance running; and the conformance suite's runtime facet turning green. **Explicitly PARKED with a reason** — recorded in DoD-4 and the Coverage Ledger: decimal and temporal representability overflow; the maxplaces compile-time precision proof; the number-model redesign; the post-runtime flag/niche layer; the definition-evolution deploy lane (how definitions evolve and migrate against already-persisted entities — confirmed out of floor scope as a future decision in its own right, decision gate GATE-N); absolute-measurement positions (the deferred new-surface question of whether to build an "instant"-analog position for units, decision gate GATE-L — carried as a deferral with a recorded reason); future-candidate proof-power (the candidate list of ten proof techniques, Karr-first — owner-gated stubs, not committed work); F-LANG-SPEC-08's ten graph modifiers; and the sign-set element-band gap (a safe over-rejection; low priority). **Nothing dropped silently.**
